===== 0002601_19061013_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Two Richest Girls in Germans-.—The Krupp wedding on October 15th, has naturally excited much interest in Germany ; the Kaiser presenting the bride, Miss Bertha Krupp, with several costly gifts. She owns the vast Krupp works at Essen, and site and her sister Barbara, are probably the two richest girls in Germany. Both became engaged to comparatively poor but well- born government officials, and the younger sister is to be married next spring. Both are good looking, with fair hair, and the typionl German countenance, and have modest and retiring manners. Had Miss Bertha fol- lowed her own inclinations, she would not have spent more than £5O on her trousseau, but, in spite of her simple tastes, her great wealth subjects her to special dangers, and, like Royalty, she has to daily submit to the presence and protection of detectives. The" Women Workers" Conferenee.—The National Union of Women Workers will hold its annual confer- ence at Tunbridge Wells, from October 22nd to 211th. The Hon. Mrs. Arthur Lyttletoss and Mrs. Bruce Glasier will both read papers on the subject of Women's Suffrage, which promises to be a most absorbing topic for discussion. Another matter to which attention will be directed is the domestic servant problem which is something of an annual feature at these conferences— although the discussions do not seem to help us forward very much in overcoming the difficulties that surround this question. Mr. George Cadbury will deal with the subject of " Technical Training " and other papers to be read include one upon "Athletics for Girls," another upon " Girl Life in Town," and several other topical features will come up for consideration.

Servant's Characters.—The wise housewife never accepts the written character of a servant if she can conveniently obtain an interview with the previous employer. That perhaps is why domestic servants in this country have never made it rule to keep a "character book" as is the universal custom in Germany. In this book the opinions and experiences of former employers are recorded in black and white, and the mistress would at once know what to think of a girl could not produce her " character book." Although we might be at first disposed to distrust such a system for fear perhaps that the identity of the girl and the book might not always be the same, yet it seems to work very well in Germany and has distinct advantages over our custom of writing on each occasion to former mistresses for sorvantscharacters.

Dearer Housekeeping.—By all accounts, winter house- keeping will be very expensive, as on all sides we hear of increased prices being demanded for provisions, coal, soap, and other necessaries. Butter, we are told, is to be fully twopence per pound dearer, and there is to be a corresponding rise in the prices of cheese and bacon. The fine and dry summer, which the southern counties in particular have enjoyed, is said to partly account for the rise in prices, which is also aided by a shortage of foreign and colonial supplies. Groceries too, are affected, and among them jams will be dearer, whilst it is declared that Christmas dried fruits will be half as much again as they were last year. Altogether, the prospect for the housewife who has to make a limited allowance go as far as possible, is not a very promising one, but it is sometimes th'e case with tradespeople as it is with dogs—their bark is worse than their bite— and they talk of a great increase in prices in order that the public may think itself well off when they ask only for a little more.

The Art of Tea-Making.—Many people who make a point of drinking one particular kind of tea and take their own supplies when they go on their summer holi- days, are surprised to find that it tastes quite different at the seaside, or wherever they may be, to what it does at home. This is, of course, due to a difference in the quality of the water, and at the recent Groceries Exhibition in London., there were some experiments shewing how important it is to have the proper blend of tea for that particular kind of water with which it is made. Hard water, and soft water, will produce a different flavour and colour, from the same tea, and it ia oialy by a judicious blending, that the best results are obtained from either. The local grocer probably does not attach due importance to whether his water comes from chalk, or from clay, but, if he does. he can secure an advantage which is not always offered to the public in the standard packet teas advertised by the big com- panies. The Lack of Patients.—The medical journals are , lamenting that the doctors every where are doing badly. There has been the usual crop of autumn fevers and ailments but influenza has not been so prevalent as in recent years, and that means a great loss of business to the doctors—to the public however, this is hardly a matter for regret—and another cause assigned for the , present bad times for the medical profession is the habit of people going to the chemist for advice, or relying upon patent medicines when out of sorts in trifling ways. Many will thialt that for this the doctors have themselves largely to blame,but in any case the impres- sion is a very general one, that they often keep attend- ing when the patient no longer needs them, and people therefok hesitate to call them in. Whether or not there is any justification for this belief, it undoubedly exists, and whilst fees and the number of dostors have both increased, in recent years, many people have dis- covered that they are none the worse for avoiding the doctor's fee. Dangerous Pashions.—ln the matter of outdoor cover- ings we have been growing more and more venturesome of late years. and this winter it will certainly not be the fault of the designers, and modistes if we escape the manifold dangers resulting from chills. The fur bolero —a dressy but dangerous garment—promises to be extensively worn by young and old alike, and the wraps that fall below the ,waist line are not likely to prove very cosy wear, when the gales and wintry weather arrive, as they art, invariably built with the cape sleeve and low in the meek. Another very risky style for this season of the year,is the new loose; threequa.rter sleeve- less coat. Several of these garments have appeared at recent fashionable weddings. For those who can afford the luxury, there is nothing to beat really well cut and ,made tailor coats and skirts, and those of us who desire I go preserve good health will see to it that the coats • .cover the hips, that the sleeves end at the wrists, and I <that necks are not cut too low.

Underskirts.—Brocade is being greatly used for petticoats, and though the out4ay is considerable, the woman who has to be economical should make an effort to possess herself of such a garment. In the end it will 'be found to be much cheaper than the familiar glace oilk petticoat, which splits so quickly and rustles so

horribly. Many women who are very particular about the.set of an underskirt sbout the hips, knit the upper part in silk on fine needles. This stretches like elastic, of course, and a perfect fit is assured. At the Milliners.—Quills, once the property of the eagle and the vulture, are in high favour and much sought after by " exclusive " dressers, and the proud tail feathers of the pheasant appear in many of the heat "creations." The majority of the plumage' however, in evidence just now, may be credited to the farmyard, though it is generally "faked "and dled beyond recog- nition. -;o far it would seem that birds are finding little favour,and we can only trust that this happy state of things will continue. MADGE.

THE PINE-MR TREATMENT.

A New Boon for Lung and Throat

Sufferers—.

"If I could breathe in enough oxygen out of the air" said a well-known physician, I could kill evegharm- ful germ in my body, and dispel every suspicion a weakness or disease about my lungs, throat, or chest." But when people are crowded together in the cities, and no longer get into contact With the pare invigor- ating atmosphere of forest and mountain? Moir health is impaired. A strain is put upon the chest, in which lie the breathing organs that support life; and colds, chills, bronchitis, consumption, pleurisy or pnemonia make inroads into the system. We cannot all go off to the famous pine forests at Europe or America immediately that hacking cough or that horrid cold seizes us. We have to continue at the scene of our labours in all weathers and under all con- ditions. Hence the discovery of a new lung and throat medicine, which virtually brings within reach of every home the identical benefits of the Pine woods of the Alps, the haunt of the robust Swiss shepherd, will be welcome news to thousands of sufferers. _

The treatment is most up-to-date, the most effective, the safest and the most agreeable for young and old. It consists of pleasant tablets, into which have been con- centrated tlie rare pine essences which account forthe health-giving odour of the famous pine woods. A tablet, having been removed from the silver paper in which iit is sealed and preserved, is crushed n the mouth or merely placed on the tongue, when the rich pine fumes are released ; and independent of the agreeable gargle provided for the throat, these fumes are breathed down the windpipe into the lungs. In this way inflammation and irritation are allayed, and phlegm is more easily ejected. Chief of all, a powerful and natural healing agency is brought into direct contact with the out-of-the-way passages of the lungs which are so often the seat of disease, and which cannot be reached by ordinary medicine swallowed into the stomach.

Peps, as these little tablets are called, are absolutely unique. They kill the germs that hide in the lungs and cause disease. Hence Peps cannot only cure the ordinary cold, but combat the worst aough, whether The cough be a symptom of bronchitis, consumption, pneumonia, pleurisy, asthma, or any lung or chest weakness. 11 Peps possess a soothing and tonic value. They are an assurance against pneumonia and pleurisy during these changeable days which are feet-wetting and lung-I chilling one moment, and warm and humid the next. No 1 one should go on a cold train-journey without having a few Peps bandy ; and:for similar reasons the remedy is convenient and useful to those who venture out thinly clad for dance or theatre at night. Peps are entirely free from opium and all other narcotics, and can be used to any extent by young or old with only the most beneficial results. Peps are equally free from the ammonia compounds, tartar- emetic, cubebs, and bromides of old-fashioned cough medicines, lozenges, and cold cures, which either affect the heart's action or irritate the stomach and kidneys. Peps are palatable and safe, and have drawn praise from the highest quarters for their unique character and splendid results in lung, throat, and chest troubles. MARCONI AND THE GRAPHOPHONE.

An Interesting Interview,

Mr. Marconi, the illustrious inventor of wireless telegraphy, who recently joined the expert staff of the Columbia Phonograph Company as consulting physicist and experimentalist, has lost no time in setting to work in hie new field of inquiry. He has just paid a visit to the Company's factory in America and taken stock of the unique resources which are there at his command, and he is now familiarising himself with the up-to-date facilities which are offered him at the Columbia laboratory in London. Mr. Marconi is enthusiastic in his new work and sanguine as to the results of his experiments. In the course .of a press interview in New York, after his inspection of the Company's American plant, he is reported ta- have said :—"I do not see that the actual mechanical con- struction of the Columbia Graphophone can be improved, but I shall conduct experiments with a view to the development of the reproducer and recorder and the effecting of economies in the making of records. I consider that the talking machine has a wonderful future and hope to do my part in bringing about its perfection." The public will naturally desire to know more about this instrument of which the famous scientist speaks so highly,and interest in this direction may be gratified by !writing to the European headquarters of the Columbia Phonograph Company, General, 89, Great Eastena-street,i London, E.C. for "Price Book 20," which gives full particulars of the Graphophone and Columbia Records which are at present engaging Mr. Marcourc attention.

A young foreign gentleman, who gave the name of Baron Soliana; and who had been staying at Fischer's Hotel, Clifford-street, London, was found on Monday, shot in the head, and was removed to the University College Hospital.

He flung off the chestnut wig that effectual.; Ly changed the whole expression of his face ; soap and water and a towel did the rest. In ten minutes he had grown ten year younger in appearance, and as he looked .at himself in the glass at the end of his opera- tions he saw an intelleetual looking young man of about twenty-eight or thirty, pale of face, rather ascetic looking, and totally different from the florid and prosperous-look- ing actor of ten minutes before. He put on a suit of blue serge and a straw hat, then he took some money from a bureau and a Smith and Wesson lievolver—it was loaded in all its five chambers—and having ex- amined the cartridges he put it. in his pocket. Then he took a card case full of cards, all with different names on them one was inscribed "The Hon. Rupert Tomlinson," another "Detective ,Inspector Bantock." another "William Andrews, coal merchant, Cold Harbour Lane, E." _ _ _

He put the card case in his pocket, looked round the room to see that everything was in its place, then he left the house. , Guided by that intuition which sometimes amounts to genius, he determined not to seek for Stirling, but for Marcas. He knew the house where Marcas lived in Howland' Street, and to Howland Street ac- cordingly he went.

No On-e,knew better than the Grafter ho W to wait and watch patiently hour by hour if needed. This time, however, luck was de- cidedly with him, for he had not taken his stand more than ten minutes at the far end of the street when he saw the cab containing John Stirling and Marcas draw up at the latter's door.

Half an hour later a strange sight might have been seen,- at Paddington Station. Strange that is to say, had you known the persons concerned. Amidst the crowd at the booking office three men stood almost touching each other in the queue of people who were waiting to take tickets.

First there was John Stirling, then Mr. Marcas, and, thirdly, the • Grafter ; all the elements of a horrible tragedy were here touching each other, yet "innocent of evil to look at as those chemicals which when com- bined and ignited produce devastating . ex- plosions., Stirling and Marcas ensconced themselves in a first-class smoking compartment. The Grafter was in the next one. He had booked to the same 'station as that for which he had heard Stirling demand two tickets ; that is to say, to Lydeard, a little station on the branch line that runs to Minehead. _

On the journey he was much exercised in his mind as toStirling's motive and destina- tion ; of one thing only he felt positive, his own motive: _

At Taunton Marcas • and his companion went into the refreshment room ;ithen they took their places in 'the Minehead thin which was drawn up at a-side plaiform. , It was dark when they reached Lydeard, and Mr. Marcas when he descended from the train had his handkerchief to his mouth after the fashion of a person who is 'afflicted with a toothache. _ _

1 The Grafter followed them to the little hotel near the station. Stirling- went in but Mr, Idarcas stayed outside.

_ Blue Anehor lies some nine miles from Lydeard, and the Grafter, as he ordered a cup of bovril at the bar, heard Stirling's voice in the passage ordering a closed carriage to be got ready at once to take him to Blue Anchor. He heard the landlord passing the order on to the ostler, and then Stirling left the hotel and joined Marcas outside.

Now all this seemed verY unaccountable to the mind of -the Grafter. Why did not Mar- cas come into the hotel; why did he hold that handkerchief to his face; why did they order a closed carriage, ? Could he have seen into the dark mind of Mr. MarcaS he would have found a ready answer to all these questions, an answer, more- over, that would have made him shiver with horror, hardened scoundrel though he was. Not being able to see into Mr. Marcas' mind he determined to find out the answer for him- self.

The landlord was passing in the passage outside. The Grafter hailed him. There was no one else in the bar, and the Grafter closing , the door took ta card case from his pocket and handed the landlord a card inscribed, "De- tective Inspector Bantock, Scotland Yard."

The landlord looked startled. "You saw that gentleman who ordered a carriage just now ? " " Yes, sir." "I. have followed him from London. lam a detective."

"Good Lord, sir, what's he done ? " "Never mind that. I must accompany him to Blue Anchor; come into the yard with me when the carriage is getting ready and I •will explain everything to you." The landlord, looking much perturbed, led the way into the stable yard where a closed, carriage had been drawn out of the coach- ol'CP and where a very unwillina horse was being led from *e stable. Ten minutes later the carriage was ready and in the road. Mr. Marcas and his eorn- panion got in, their bags were handed in, and they started, little dreaming that the coach- man muffled up on the box was their deadliest enemy, the never forgetting, never forgiving Grafter.

The \ road from Lydeard to Blue Anchor runs through a wild and beautiful country. On one side lie the Quantock Hills, the home of the red deer, on the other the Brencllms. , It is as lonely a road as one could wish to meet with or to avoid and very hilly in parts. They were more than half on their way in the centre of a stretch of road that lay like a white ribbon in the uncertain starlight when the Grafter suddenly pulled up. The next moment he was on the ground re- volver in hand; the next he had torn the car- riage door open with all the fury of revenge about to be satisfied.; the next he had dropped his revolver with a cry of horror. There was only one person in the carriage, a dusky figure that seemed nodding and laugh- ing at him,' '

Marcas whistled. "I'm lost," said Stirling, gazing • blankly before him with an expression of terror on his face. " Oh, nonsense," replied Marcas ; "it's not so bad as that." This has determined me, the place is too hot for me. I'll go to America." "Have you enough money for such a journey? " "Plenty. The bother is how to leave the country." " Lots," said Mr. Marcas, whose total capital at that moment amounted to some • three pounds odd. "Well," said Stirling, "let's be off. I must go to my rooms to collect my things. Outside Stirling hailed a hansom ; they got in, and he told the driver to take them to Rupert Street. Half way up Rupert Street they stopped. "My rooms are here," said Stirling; "come in and help me to pack." He led the way upstairs to a small suite of rooms on the second floor consisting of a bed- room, dressing-room, and sitting-room open- ing one off another. • Here Stirling packed a huntinr, hit bag with everything he thought needful, then he opened a desk and took out a bundle of bank notes. • Marcas seated smoking cigarettes watched his companion like, a cat. lien Stirling went into his bedroom for rive minutes and re- turned dressed in a suit of grey tweed and a bowler hat. He took some gold from his pocket and placed two sovereigns and a half sovereign on the table. " Now," he said, " I'm ready." "What are you leaving the money on the table for? " asked Marcos. "Its due to my landlady." " But; surely to goodness--" "I know, but I don't rob landladies, its un- lucky. Come." ' The hansom was waiting for them and they got in. " I want you' to stop at my place in How- land Street," said Alarms. " I want to get a bag and some things." ' All right,", said Stirling, " we have plenty of tine." At Howland Street Alarms alighted, and leaving his companion in the cab, opened the door of the house where he -lived, with a latch key. He ran upstairs to the miserable room where he lodged on the top floor, packed a few things into a black bag, and then went to an old portmanteau in one corner of the room. He unlocked it, fumbled amidst its contents and then drew out a knife. It was a sheath knife. He drew the blade from the sheath. It was six inches long, double edged, keen, and cruel-looking. He replaced it 11n its sheath and placed it in his pocket. Then he picked up his bag and ran downstairs. "You haven't been long," said; Stirling as they drove off. "Oh, I was long enough for what I wanted," replied Alarms with a smile that would have frozen his companion's heart could he have known what it implied.

He put his hand to his head as if to make; sure of his own personality and of the fact that he was not in a dream.

The situation was sufficiently amusing from a certain point of view, and that the greatest thief in the world should be robbed in his turn seemed just enough. But Lord de Morley was not thinking of the humorous or just side of the business; he only felt that the thing which he had fancied to be safe within his grasp was gone.

Now the Grafter, instead of flinging the pieces of newspaper away and stamping upon the biscuits as another man might have done, held them in his hand and stared at them as his swiftly working memory flew back like a hound over the last few hours.

".I have it," he cried suddenly. "It was

"A thief earned John Stirling. These bis- cuits have told me all." "Those biscuits? "

"You must know, my lord," said the 'Grafter, who was now quite cool," that in the parcel with the document I had placed a jewel, a large opal which I had hoped also to sell back to you. Coming here this morning I stopped at several bars in the Strand ; 'the parcel was stolen from me at White's bar, the opal and the document taken from it, and some pieces of newspaper and these two bis- cuits enclosed in it, then the percel was put back in my pocket. These two biscuits com- bined are about the same size and bulk as the opal."

"But how do you know that you were robbed at White'e ? "

For answer the Grafter held out the biscuits. They were little biscuits about the size of a penny, such as in many restaurants and bars in London are placed on the counter with cheese. On each biscuit was stamped. " White, Strand.'!

" Now," said the injured Grafter, " I have a clue to the whole thing. There are only half a dozen men in London who could have robbed me like that. In White's this morning they were all betting men except one, John Stir- ling, the prince of all thieves." • " But, said de Morley suspiciously, " I don't see why this man, if he robbed you, need have put two .biscuits into the parcel before he replaced it in your pocket:" "

"He put 'them in to-.inake up the bulk, of the opal; he guessed pretty well that I would be occasionally putting my hand into my pocket to see if the parcel was safe, and that if 1 found it-wasn't the same bulk I'd twin, every- thing at once,' may be immediately I'd left the bar. He hates me, but he has a very big dread of me."

"Now, I'll tell you exactly what he did. He got the parcel from me. opened it, took out the contents with his back turned towards me, looked round for something to place in it in their stead, saw the little biscuits on the counter, wrapped them in two strips torn from the ' Sporting Tirnes,' did up the parcel, and replaced it /in my pocket. All done in two minutes." •

" At all events," said de Morley, " this man Stirling is only a common thief. He cannot decipher or make use of the document most likely he'll throw it away or destroy it." " I'm not so sure of that," said -the Grafter, "What made him rob me? He must have known that I had something of great value d.. upon me or he'd never have dare Has your lordship been approached by any other parties but me, concerninff the lost jewels? "

"Why, yes, that reminds me, a man called the other day at my club offering to help to find them, a man called Marcus, or Marcas, or some such name."

"A mafi called who ? " cried the ,Grafter, forgetting grammar in his excitement.

• " Marcas." "1 see it all now I Oh. the scamp 1* " Do you know this man Marcas ?."

"I should think I did." "Do you know where he lives ? " . _ _ _ _

"I do," said the Grafter, buttoning up his coat and seizing hold of his hat. "I do, and not a moment is to be lost. I must be on his track. Ah, the scoundrel! the sneak-"

" Stop," said Lord de Morley ; " it is essen- tial that this document should be recovered at any price." "My lord," said the Grafter, "we agreed upon a sum n just now; will you promise to give me it when I place the document in your hands ? " • " Yes."

" I believe in your word, and now I must go, every moment is of importance. I will communicate with your lordship at the vary earliest opportunity." " You will, at least, leave me your real name and your address ? " "My lord, it would be useless. Believe me that I will do my best. There is such a thing as revenge, and if I don't have it on this sneak —thief—within twenty-loer hours my name's not—"

He stopped, suddenly and smiled, then he made for the door and a inonient later had left the house.

" Well," Said de Morley. as he stood alone in the library cogitating over this strange interview, " if that man is not an out and out villain I am strangely mistaken, and it seems that I, a peer of England, have made a con- tract with him. Yet I had to do it." Desperate remedies are required in desperate diseases, and I am doing it not only for 'my own sake, but for the 'sake of Harding and several other people as well."

THE BRAVE CAT.

A lady's only son was once taken very ill with fever. Danger being past, he was allowed to lie on the sofa in the parlour' wrapped up in blankets. The lady left him for a few minutes to go and see to the icooking. In the meanwhile the little boy had fallen asleep, and,in his sleep he had turned over. Consequently he fell on the floor, the sofa being narrow, the blankets at the same time twisting round his neck, and choking him. Now this little boy had a cat whom he dearly loved and never did it any harm, and the cat in return loved its master. This cat had never left the boy's bedside once, and seeing his master's danger, he ran to the lady of the house and scratched her hand, and did the same thing over and over again. The lady, thinking something was the matter, ran In, and found her boy nearly choked to death. ,The boy's life was saved by the cat. ' True is the motto, "Be kind to dumb animals, and they in return will be kind to you."

From your loving niece, MAY COLINILL.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l am sending a contribu- tion to the "Children's Corner," which I have composed myself. Please accept my best thanks, for the delicious chocolates which you sent me. Hoping my story, which i 4 an auto- biography, will gain a prize, I remain, your loving niece, ELSIE LEWIS. THE AUTOBIOGRAPHY OF A SLIPPER.

I am an old dancing-slipper, but I was not always old, ragged, and useless. I was made in Paris, and I can remember when I was placed in the window of my master's shop for sale. The satin which I am made of was then e beautiful pink colour o and I, had large silver buckles on my toes. ; _ Then ,an English gAitleman bought me, 'to give to his little daughter for a birtliday present, and oh! wasn't she pleased when she saw me! he kissed me in her deligh.t, and stroked my satin. When my little mistress wore me at her first ball, I was admired by all her co mpanions, and I can tell you my buckles shone that night. My little mistress wore me a.t many dances after that, and wasn't I proud! Bilk alas! now I am old and worn, and I am of no tige to anyone. My once beautiful satin is now ragged and faded, and I have lost both my handsome buckles, and I am a sight to behold! My mistress has now forgotten me. I often wonder what will become of me when 1 am ,turned cut of the cupbo'ard where I have been for years. When the other shoes and slippers laugh at nay ragged condition, I remind them that I was once covered with pink satin and had real silver buckles on my toes.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l am afraid you will think am not a very industrious member, but I am sorry I have forgotten you. I hope you will like the piece of poetry I am sending you. With fond love, I remain,

WITHIN AN OLD AND SMOKY TOW:

Within an old and -smoky town There stood a narrow street, And down it came a little girl, With weary, naked feet. Her clothes were old and very poor, . With many a ragged place, And fast ran down the streaming tears Upon poor Annie's face. I cannot tell you cAly she cried; Perhaps she wanted food, Perhaps some bigger, stronger child Had been unkind or rude. But so she went, with slow, sad steps, All down that dirty street, And not one kind or pitying word Did little Annie meet. TJnfil another ragged child Looked from a window high, Down, down into that street, And saw poor Annie cry. This little girl was very poor, And had no money of fier own; Her greatest treasure was a rose, Which bore one bud alone. But with a loving, pitying thought, She plucked the . rosebud sweet _ And dropped it from the window ledge, Down just at Annie's feet. The weeping child forgot the tears That stood within her eyes, And with a happy smile She 'picked up her new-found prize. She smelt it o'er and o'er again, And smoothed each ruffled leaf, And in her pleasure quite forgot • About her former grief. She had not seen the rose-bud fall, , Nor turned her glance on high To see the fase that looked so pleased Because she did not cry. But, surely, God. Who loves the heart, And lovingly bestows, Smiled on the unselfish litle girl That gave her treasured rose.

QUEEN OF THE MAY.

Once upon a time there was a little girl named Marjory. She lived with her grand- mother in a dear little cottage in the country. Just now she was aglow with excitement, for to-morrow was the first of May. She went to bed that night early, so as to be up in good 1 time on the morrow. All of a sudden she jumped up. What was this? Where was she? Why 1 in the most beautiful garden she had ever seen, surrounded by a bed of lilies of the ' valley. But listen; what was this lovely music she heard? 'And look what was happening. Out of each leaf stepped a dainty little fairy. They proceeded to form a ring around her, and they were chanting a mysterious song. Sud- denly they stopped and said to her, "You are going to be Queen of the May." They started to sing again, and slowly departed to their homes (the lily leaves) once more. Marjory awoke with a start, and saw the sun streaming in her window. She hastily dressed and went out in the meadows to pick some flowers.," ' While there her coFupanions pounced upon her, ir and, putting a wreath upon her head, told he she was Queen ef the May. ,Marjory was quite,mo, happy, you may be sure, for she had often longed to be Queen of the May. ..:#Ol6 4, Dear Uncle Ned —I received your bottle of • Extract of Herbs, which I must thank you for 4 .a very much. NOVEMBER ELECTIONS.

- - -- The CHAIRIAAN explained that the Executive Com- mittee had considered the question of contestin* seats at the coming November elections and had decided to run Mr. J. Morton in Bewsey Ward. The action of the Committee was confirmed. VISIT OF MR. HAVELOCK WILSON M.P. Mr. J. HOWES explained that the League were next Thursday to be favoured with a visit from Mr.Havelock Wilson, M.P., who will address a meeting in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street. It was hoped that the meeting would be a success and that the committee would be able to hand a substantial sum to the treasurer of the fund to indemnify Mr. Wilson from the action of the Shipping Federation in the enforcing of a judg- ment for libel obtained against him several years ago. Mr. Howes said Mr. Wilson had had to fight an up-hill battle on behalf of the seamen and had received very little financial support from the men themselves. It would be regrettable indeed if the progressive forces of the country allowed him to suffer as the result of his advocacy of the course of labour. There was no doubt that the money required to indemnify him would be forthcoming but it was hoped to spread it over as wide an area as possible and to include thousands of small contributions. (Hear, hear.) Mr. S. WRIGHT thought the League ought to take the matter up. It had been said that they had no right to call themselves a labour league but they could show the people who said that that they were thoroughly in earnest and that they had the interest of labour at heart. (Applause.) A number of members agreed to take out collecting books on behalf of the fund.

THE LABOUR POLICY OF THE GOVERNMENT.

WORKINGMEN MAGISTRATES.

Mr. BARNES in a brief speech referred to the labour policy of the Government. He explained how the Government had made it possible for their employees in the Post Office, Royal Dock Yards and War Office to combine and approach the heads of the department with respect to any grievances which they might have. In dealing with the unemployed the Tory Government male a pretence of doing something. The Liberal party did not make a pretence but did something and had allocated £200,000 to be used for the benefit of the unemployed. (Applause.) Mr., JOSEPH WOODS thought that the Government had not given the workingman sufficient opportunities to be the judge of the working man. (Hear, bear.) Mr.E. W. Jos said that as had been demonstrated very clearly in the local papers the policy of the Liberal party was the real policy of the Labour party. He thought the Labour party were making a mistake in asking for the whole loaf and that they would do better if, like the Liberal Party, they accepted half a loaf and then asked for more. (Hear, bear). Mr. S. WRIGHT said it was 14 years since he was appointed as a workingman magistrate or the Borough and since then there had not been another working man placed on the Bench, and be thought that when auy new mngistrates were appointed he ought to have at least one colleague. During the whole of the time that he had been on the Bench he had never been nominated by either Liberals or %ries for a seat on the Licensing Committee, whereas hk, thought that as one who had been associated with the town and who had lived in it all his life he was quite as capable of judging the requirements of the workingmen as any gentleman who had lived outside- the town and had little local knowledge. (Applause.) One of the prin- cipvl changes which he had witnessed while he had been on the Bench was that the magistrates were beginning to have a due regard to the environment of the prisoners who were brought before them. Environment was the Call,o of a great deal of the crime and he would like to see the time when it would be possible for the Corporation to advance to a workingman the money with which to build a house for himself. How could they possibly expect intelligent human beings to come out of districts such as Dial-street? It was not to be expected that persons comb from such neighbourhoods would take an intelligent interest i,n the affairs of the town. (Applause). Mr. HOWES said that in a very short time new magis- trates would be appointed in Warrington on the recom- mendation of the Member for the Borough, and it was their place to at once suggest names of suitable workingmen to Mr. Crosfield. They must not blame Mr. Crosfield if he went wrong, unless they informed him of the course which they wished him to take in the I matter. With regard to unemployment, they must remember that employment by itself was not all that they wanted, but they must see that the employment was profitable. It had been urged that men did not want charity but work—unfortunately there Was a great army who would have charity rather than work—and he agreed that to a man who wanted it, it was almost a crime for him to be out of employment. If there was one man who could solve the question it was Mr. John Burns. The president of the Local Government Board had a knowledge of the class of people with whom they had to deal and he thought that before long as the result of the labours of Mr. Burns, they would find a very great difference with the regard to the opportunities for employment for those who would work and that proper treatment would be meted out to those who would not work. (Applause). Mr. MORTON expressed his pleasure at the excellent attendance and hoped that throughout the session they would have large gatherings so that they could educate themselves to flight the great politica menace—Socialism—for the sake of the working classes themselves. (Hear, hear). A brief reply by the chairman concluded the meeting. At next week's meeting Mr. Morton will deliver an I address on " Some socialistic fallacies."

The Manchester District Peace Society.

This Society is to be inaugurated at a meetinffsto he held in the Memorial Hall, Albert-square Manleter, on 17th inst., when Sir William Mather, ',LP., L.L.D., will be the principal speaker. The Society will be, affiliated to the Peace Society and will take the place of its Manchester Auxiliary. There has already been a very hearty response to the appeal of the Committee, about 70 having joined as annual subscribers. The Secretary, Mr. Charles Stevenson, 9, Albert-square, will be glad to correspond with any in the district, which includes Preston, Clitheroe and Caine in the North; Todtnorden, Rochdale Oldham, Stalybridge, and Buxton on the East ; Macclesfield and Crewe on the South ; and Warrington and Wigan on the West, and all towns within that area. It is intended to hold nieeiings all over the district, and a strenuous effort will be made to combat militarism in every shape and form. All willing to help are invited to write to the Society.

DANGEROUS DEBILITY.

Bile Beans Restore Vigorous Health

to an Ailing Mother.

Debility means general weakness of the whole system, and is frequently the result of functional derangement of the liver, stomach, and bowels. At this time of the year debility is particularly dangerous, for it leaves an open door to many prevailing epidemics. Bile Beaus tone up and invigorate the whole system, and strengthen it to withstand sickness and disease. I Mrs. Emily Beevers, of 5, Carlton-street, Castleford- road, Normanton, has told a reporter of the "Normanton Gazette "how Bile Beans restored her to robust health. "For ten long months I wished every day I was . dead," she said. 'Everything seemed wrong with me. Every meal I had came back again and I hated the sight of even a drop of milk. I could not even bear the noise of the children. I used to feel dizzy and faint and got very weak. When I walked I gasped every step I took, and the pains in my head were so bad I had to cover my eyes up. My skin turned a nasty ye low colour. " The doctor said that ana,,mia was at the bottom of it all. I tried almost every kind of medicine, but nothing did me good for long. After my baby was born I was a month in bed taking doctor's medicine, but I did not pick up very well. "I got a small box of Bile Beans, but I did not persevere with them. Then my husband went and got me some more medicine, but it was just like the other —it did me good for a time and then I was as bad as ever. After this my husband got me some more Bile Beans and made me follow up the treatment. The pains in my head went away, the sickness passed off. Now I can eat almost anything, and I am never without a box of Bile Beans in the house. They have quite cured me." Bile Beans for Biliousue.ss are sold, by all chemists, at is. Id. or 2s. H. per box.

WOMAYS REALM.

DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

AUTUMN CLOTHES.

There is somethin.g very pleasant about the first warm clothes of autumn, says a Globe fashion writer; they are cosy and agreeable, new, becoming, and have not the discomfort of real cold to take away from our enjoyment of them. Dark clothes are, on the whole, more becoming to the majority of people than light. They are less trying to the skin, and they encourage the contrasting brightness of the hair. The texture, also, soft and thick, is very becoming. Flecked tweeds seem to be coming in again, and large dark checks also. Exceedingly smart is a cos- tume of dark blue checked in squares of an inch and a-half with black, the material being smooth cloth, and the only irregularity being a few flecks of black on the edge of the black squares. The skirt is arranged in groups a box pleats, stitched down half-way to the knee, and thence falling flat, but with plenty of fan-like fulness to give with every motion. The deep hem is closely machine-stitched, to give it stiffness. and the upper edge of the hem is outlined with a narrow band of black astrakhan on each side of an inch- wide black velvet insertion. The coat is outlined round the base of the neck and down each side of the front by a similar arrangement of velvet-

SERVANT QUESTION IN NEW ZEALAND.

Over all home life throughout New Zealand, writes Constance Barnicoat in the Lady, lies the shadow of the Servant Question—a black shadow in England, but blacker beyond measure on the other side of the world. Here in England 30u do not know what servant troubles are; you should have the Colonial servant for a little while. To such a pass has she come that many New Zealand families, rather than put up with her ways, do without her altogether. The Colonial mistress must give her maid a number of hours off and an amount of holidays unheard of in England, and often passing all reason. I know one charming family who can never keep a servant. And why? Merely because they have eight o'clock dinner, Nj(hich the servants con- sider too late. Moreover, the Colonial servant, when you get her, barely knows her business. She is anything but what in England would be called a trained servant." On the other hand, she certainly does far more work than an Eng- lish servant, and she is probably honest. In New Zealand there is not any feeling on the part of servants against their mistresses do- ing household work. The mistress must, else the work would never get done. Moreover, they must be, and, I think, usually are, quick and competent housewives, and also often ad- mirable cooks.

Even with such a scarcity of servants, many things are done in a New Zealand home that are often put out in England.. The Colonial mis- tress, who must, on occasion, be able to be her own maid-of-all-work, has the washing done at home, and often the laundry work. She gene- rally makes her own jams and bottles her own fruits, and she makes her own scones and cakes, and has very good ones. Only a small propor- tion of people keep more than two servants, the great majority only one. Work must therefore be simplified.

CLOTH COSTUME.

The original of the ' costume sketched was worn by a smart American woman. It was made of fine blue faced cloth, the long, closely-fitting coat being finished with a roll collar, which met only at the waist. This was of ve)vet closely set with rows of machine-stitching, and the buttons with whi•-•1- the coat fastened were also velvet- covered. The waistcoat, with its invisible fasten-

ings, was of white cloth, braided with two shades of blue silk cord. The coat was so long that only a few inches of skirt were visible underneath, just sufficient to see it was of the same material. This model would look very well copied in Bur- gundy-red faced cloth, with a small ' velvet hat tc match, trimmed with a couple of ostrich plumes, placed one on either side.

SORE HANDS

Painful Cracking and Scaly Roughness Cured by Zam-Buk.

Working wives and breadwinners are liable to suffer from a pecnliar form of eczema in the hands. The skin becomes hot, dry, and sore, afterwards breaking out in painful, irritating cracks and fissure-like sores, which are sometimes accompanied by swelling and inflamma- tion. _ _ .

Zam-Buk is very soothing and softening for this trouble. It stops the irritation, takei out the tender- ness and soreness, and finally restores the members to a normal condition of health, growing new, tort clear skin. Mrs. T . THE INFIRMARY CONCERT.

The Workingmen's Committee of the Warrington Infirmary and Dispensary held their annual concert on behalf of the funds of the institution in the Parr Hall on Saturday evening. It was gratifying to see the effort so generously supported by the public, the hall being well filled in every part. As is usual at these concerts, the programme was an interesting one and the audienct, was cordial in its appreciation. The members of Mr. T.J.Down's orchestra once More placed their services at the disposal of the committee and,under the conductor- ship of Mr. Arthur Down, gave several pleasing selec- tions.lt was early noticeable that there woe a considerable improvement in the tone and balance of the various instruments. Most of all must we congratulate Mr. Down on the subjection of the brass wind which in itself,- and were no other improvement apparent, is a great point gained, for the strings and the lighter wind instruments were in a negative way much better able to assert their proper and premier position and to thereby do justice to much of the music essayed. The" Pique Dame" overture (Suppog) and Sullivan's Shakespearian incidental music were the best of the orchestral pieces all of which.howewer,evinced more than the band's usual degree of taste and all of which were listened to with interest and apparent pleasure. Miss Marie Houghton fully justified the high opinion which Warrington concert-goers have formed of her vocalabilities. Her sweet and flexible voice found full scope in "The Old Green Isle" (Temple) and " The Nightingale" (Batton) both Of which she sang with taste and expression. Miss Bessie Eckersley sang "Angus MacDonald" (Roecloel) and "Laud of Hope and Glory" (lilgar) in a contralto voice of fine quality especially in the upper register, and her unaffected earnestnPss in interpreting these songs earned for her well-deserved plaudits. Mr. William Wild (Stalybridge) possesses a pure-toned tenor voice with which be interpreted very feelingly "My Queen" (Blumenthal), and The Maid of Malabar" (Adams). Each of these items was vocalised in the best of taste and called forth warm applause. We were particularly pleased with the singing of Mr. J. H. Ditchburn (Chester) who submitted The Vicar's Song" from the Gilbert- Sullivan Opera "The Sorcerer" and "Still is the Night" (Abt). He has a base voice of fine Dower and quality and he fully brought out the spirit of the com- positions. The audience was delighted and accorded a hearty encore. An enjoyable feature of the secomi half of the programme was the humorous duet "The two beggars " (W ilson) which was interpreted in spirited style by Messrs. Wild and Ditchburn. The humorous element, supplied by Mr. Mark Tvme, was not of a very high order. Mr. W. Payton, Mus. Bac. was an able accompanist.

LArCHFORb ICOLBB.

I Latchford met Eccles at Eccles on Saturday: 'ThO ' home team eon the toss and took advantage of a s/ight I slope in the ground. Play wiss fast, and Cliffe had hard lines in the circle, the ball striking.,the goal post. End to end play ensued but neither team could put on the finishing touch. Eccles scored just before the interval. Early in the second half, Cliffe was hurt and had to retire. This weakened the Latchford attack, but they stuck manfully to their task. Callon missed the goal twice by inches, in a very short time. From a free hit I near the Eccles 25, Latchford, by a smart bit of passing I scored. Latchford were still keeping up the pressure (when the whistle went fortime and the game ended in a draw of one goal each. • LatchfOrd second renewed their acquaintance with Eccles second at Latchford. From the start the home team outplayed their opponents and put on 4 goals in the first half throngh the instrumentality of Hackney, Banks, and Allender; the' first having 2 to his credit. After the interval Latchford still pressed and Minards managed to score 5, an Latchford ran out winners by 5 goals to nil.

WARRINGTON TRADESMEN'S ASSOCIATION.

The Competition from the Market.

MR. W. RODERTS AND PROPERTY PURCHASING.

I The annual meeting of the Warrington and District Tradesmen's Association was held at Toft's Assembly Rooms, Bridge-street, on Tuesday evening under the presidency of Arr. W. P. There was a fair attendance, including Mr. W. Roberts, LP. (who in the absence of Mr. Woolhouse through illness, acted as secretary) and Mr. J. Banks (trelsurer). The 01:19.IRM&N expressed the sincere regret of the members at the illness of Mr. Woolhouse who, he said, had worked most energetically on behalf of the Association. He also read a letter of apology for non- attendance from Mr. James Critchley. •

===== 0002601_19061013_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ANNUAL REPORT.

The SECAETARY'S report was submitted. It stated that the committee would impress upon the members of the association the absolute necessity there is for attendance at the quarterly meetings, if the association is to become the powerful organization it is capable of becoming. There are many questions of the highest moment discussed at thee meetings, and matters are placed before the members of the utmost importance to tradesmen and the town generally, but these are of such a character that it is impossible to deal with them in a report of this description. Several times has your committee felt it its duty to communicate with various committees of the Corpora- tion, and it is happy to record the fact that its sugges- tions have received consideration, in some cases favourably. A concession was made by the Tramways Committee In the way of issuing tokens to errand boy's. The Watch Committee was appealed to regarding Sunday Trading in the borough, and a resolution ,was forwarded on this subject to Lord Avebury, chairmin of the Royal Commission on Sunday Trading. This is a matter which your committee looks upon with grave concern, and trusts that as an outcome of the Royal Commission legislation will ensue which will, at all events, con- siderably mcdify this evil of Sunday trading. Much discussion, both in committee and at general meetings, centred round the suggested alteration of Walking Day. It came to the knowledge of the Executive that an attempt was being made to alter the date of Walking Day to the Friday preceding Whit-Monday. After going into the matter and making inquiries of several trades- men, the committee proposed that if it was considered advisable to interfere with present arrangements, the Friday preceding August Bank Holiday would be the most convenient day. Representation was made to the School Attendance Committee, and this committee having considered the question, passed a resolution in accordance with the views expressed by the association but the Education Committee declined to confirm the resolution. The various works in the town were then appealed to and 32 letters sent out; 31 replies were received, each firm expressing its opinion and giving reasons for or against any change. The numbers for and against were about equal, but the larger works prefer the arrangement at present in force. The Association, in face of these replies, decided to take no further action this year, hut to keep the ques- tion before it. One great achievement of the .Associa- tion has been the formation of the Plate Glass Insur- ance Company, which will undoubtedly prove very advantagesus to Hie members of the Association. It is a branch of the Association which your committee hopes wilbesupported by every member. Mr. W. Roberts, Mr. J. E. Bichardson, and Mr. J. Critchley attended an inquiry into the borrowing of £7,000 for electrical pur- poses. Mr. Richardson put some very pertinent ques- tions, and was complimented by the Inspector. The annual horse parade was fixed to take place on the Saturday preceding Whit Monday. It was thought by your committee that this would seriously interfere with business on what is usually a busy day. Steps were at once taken with the result that the demonstration was postponed to the following week. Much could be ; said on the markets question, but the committee need I only say that it is satisfied with the progress that is being made. A deputation consisting of Mr. W. Roberts and Mr. F. Pendlebury appeared before the Markets Committee owing to its having been reported that an extension of the market was contemplated. The com- mittee gave a very patient and considerate hearing to the deputation, and assured Messrs. Roberts and Pendle- bury that no extension would take place at present. This assurance, it need hardly be said, gave great satis- faction.

THE CHAIRMAN AND THE MARKET COMPETITION.

In proposing the adoption of the report, the CHAIRMAN said the one thing which had pleased him more than anything else was the exceedingly happy and united feeling which had been displayed amongst the members of the association. When they remembered that there were now over a hundred of the principal tradesmen in the association, comprising gentlemen of all political and religious beliefs, who had gathered together to consider matters which were for the best interests of the town it was a matter for congratulation that such an admirable feeling prevailed. He thought that the question which affected the tradesmen most was the contemplation of making further extensions to their already very large market. It was, he believed, pretty well understood amongst the town's tradesmen that they had very serious competion in the market, and a competition which they did not think was always altogether fair. They did not wish to be, in any way, antagonistic, but wanted fair and legitimate competi- tion, and then they would be afraid of neither the market, the co-operative stores, nor any other combina- tion. (Hear, hear.) The size of the market was more than ample for the requifements of the borough for many years to come, and they hoped that before any extension took place the re-arrangement of the present market would meet all requirements. The formation of the Plate Glass Insurance Company was a goad thing, and it was evidently appreciated by the tradesmen. (Hear, hear). Mr. J. E. RICHARDSON, in seconding, referred with regret to the Secretary's illness, and proceeded to observe that the members had every reason to congratu- late themselves upon the result of the year's working. Several members of the Town Council were members of the Association, and the interchange of views could not but prove of value. The resolution was carried. I THE PRESIDENT. On the proposition of Mr. GORST, seconded by Mr. BANKS, Mr. W. Roberts was unanimously elected president in succession to the late Mr. Joseph Shaw, whose death was referred to with great regret. THE CORPORATION'S PROPERTY PURCHASES. The PRESIDENT in returning thanks hoped that the honour of the presidency would go round, so that each of his colleagues might at some time occupy the position. He went on to say that a matter that was creating a serious division of opinion in the town was the purchasing of property by .the Corporation, and he would like to give his own opinion on the question. He thought that a great deal of dissatisfaction and unrest would be removed if the Property Purchasing Committee of the Corporation, when they had made a provisional agreement to purchase, would as soon as possible after making that agreternent, take the General Purposes Committee, which consisted of the whole Council, into their confidence. (Hear, hear). In that way a great deal of ill-feeling and suspicion would be removed. He was not going to offer an opinion with regard to the proposed purchase of Colonel Edelsten'a property in Knutsford-road, for the simple reason that he did not know anything about the value of that propert7l It would be therefore most unfair for him -to Criticize the proposed purchase ; but he understood that that agreement to purchase had been made many months ago and that it was only quite recently that the members of the Council had been acquainted with the fact. He could not See why, after that agree, ment had been entered into, this matter was not fully 'reported upon ti the very next Meeting of the General Purposes tiommittee by the committee that had to deal with the matter, and then decided either far or against: fit thought if that had Veen done, and the Came with °thee forties Which had been purchased, thefe would have been Very little cause for complaint. Speakind generally with tericard to the list of pnrchases of p report,' which was published a short time age, he ventured to say that a considerable number of those purchases had been greatly to the advantage of the borough, and he thought the Corporation was to be congratulated upon many of those purchases. While many were ready to criticise, he thought there would be others equally ready to give credit to the Corporation when it had made a good bargain in the interests of the ratepayers. He believed that if such a procedure as he now suggested were adopted much of the unpleasantness and bad feeling which seemed to exist in their Town Council would be removed. Personally he was strongly of the opinion that this continued bitterness and bad feeling which seemed to be displayed in the Council was not to the interest of the Corporation or to the credit of their good old town—and he hoped that when the Cor- poration had been strengthened by the introduction of the new blood which he trusted would be added on November Ist. the cause of a lot of that suspicion and distrust would disappear, and that the good and fair name of Warrington would be restored once again to the credit of all concerned. (Applause). OTHER OFFICERS.

Other officers were elected as follows :—Vice-presi- dents, Messrs. F. Pendlebury, H. N. Houghton, J. E. Richardson, James Critchley, W. T. Williams and P. Gorst ; Treasurer, Mr. Banks ; Secretary Mr. Wool- house ; Committee, Councillors Evans and Lord,Messrs. W. T. Williarns,P. J. Gorst,J. H. Houghton,J. Critchley, C. Toft, F. Pendlebury, J. W. Hall; W. Isherwood, J. W. Holt, W. Taylor, T. Shaw and W. Cooper. The Secretary and Treasurer were cordially thanked for their past services. FINANCE.

The Treasurer's report showed that the association liad to its credit a balance of £4 3a.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES. Wycliffe P.S.A. Society. There was a good attendance on Sunday at the meet- ing of this Society. Mr. Joseph Owen presided. Mr. James Shaw, of Warrington, was the speaker, and based his address upon" Ye must be born again." He bore testimony to the good done by ,P.S.A. Societies, and said the effects could not be otherwise, with the sing- ing, prayers, and addresses which were given. Miss E, Haviour sang sweetly " Behold Me standing at the door" and Hold Thou mina hand." Mr. Payton, Mos. Bac. presided at the organ. The Secretary (Mr. B- Farrar; welcomed four new members, making a total of 487. The contributions to the Organ Fund for the day amounted to £1 9s. About 330 prize books were distributed.

St. Mary's Church, Buttermarket-street. Blessing of the Bells. I There 'was a crowded congregation at S. Mary's (R.C.) Church, Buttermarket-street, on Sunday evening ; when the bells which are to be hung in the new tower I were blessed by thq Abbot of Ampleforth. The whole of the evening was devoted to the ceremony which was performed with due dignity. Vested in a white cope, ;with mitre and pastoral staff, and attended by the clergy lof the church, the Abbot approached the bells, which ! were ranged in front of the altar milsovhen a number of psalms were recited. After the salt and water had been exorcised the Abbot commenced the washing of the bells, the aloe being continued by the attendant ministers whilst psalms of praise were recited. The sign of the cross was then made on each bell with the holy oil and subsequently seven crosses were made out- side the bells with the holy oil and four with the Chrism inside, the Abbot meanwhile saying" May this signal be blessed and consecrated, 0 Lord, in the Name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost." The placing of incense underneath the hells concluded the ceremony which, though somewhat lengthy, was followed with great interest. During the proceedings an appropriate sermon was delivered by the Rev. D. C. CUMMINS, 0.5.8. Many and various said, the preacher, were the voices with which Almighty God spoke to his children. Every- thing strong and mighty and glorious in the world, everything beautiful and inspiring and generous were to those who were able to listen the voice of God. The voices of the bells which were that night being blessed, could truly be spoken of as the voice of God and let them not harden their hearts to the iron tongues of the bells which would speak to them and to their children's children unto many generations of the one thing necessary ; which would proclaim for ever, in the midst of a heedless world, to them all that they were servants of God. On the silence of the Sabbath the voices of the bells would steal over their sleep telling them gently of the Divine command, first spoken by God to lkloses in the midst of thunder and lightening, to keep holy the Sabbath Day, and would call them together to fall down and worship the Lord. At eventime the bells would not speak with the voice of command but the voice of invitation and welcome. At other times, at sunrise and sunset, they would ring out proclaiming the glad tidings of great joy making it known to them as the voice of the angels made it first known to the shepherds at Bethlehem, and of the tremendous and glorious mystery of the Incarnation of God's Son by which their salvation had been achieved. In the church had been erected a tablet bearing the inscription: "Pray for the good estate of all who to God's honour and glory helped to build the tower of St. Mary's and likewise of those who in their generosity gave the bells, viz. :—St. Mary's a benefactor ; St. Benedict's a benefactor: St. Wilfrid's (Father Stunner's) many contributions ; St. Patrick's, Mr. A. H. Crosfleld, M.P. ; St. Richard's, Mr. T. R. Shepherd ; St. Anne's, a benefactor ; St. John's, Mr. J. Richardson and family ; St. Helen's, Mr. and Mrs. H. Houghton; whose piety may God rew trd, Amen; A.D. 1906." St. John's Presbyterian Church. Sunday last was observed as the. annual Harvest Thanksgiving at St. John's Presbyterian Church, when the preacher morning and evening was the Rev. J. L. Milne, B.D. minister of the congregation. There were good attendances at each service, especially in the evening when Mr. Milne chose for his text the story of the healing of the ten lepers as narrated in the 17th chapter of St. Luke's Gospel. It was, said the preacher , one of the instances which showed theta the depressing and discouraging nature of Christ's work on earth. Let them consider the condition of the lepers. Their desease was a terrible one, totally incurable, and it made its victims a loathsome sight. In addition to that they were social outcasts, cut off even from their own homes and families, and suffering thus it was hard for them to believe in the goodness of God.

What Other People Say.

In answer to their appeal Jesus healed them and only one returned to give Him thanks. That was so true to life ; thankfulness was so rare a spirit, and it was specially rare in taa experience of Jesus. Jesus would have been glad of the thanks of those whom be healed. He was thankful when the one leper returned; how glad He would have been had they all corns back. Jesus felt man's ingratitude still.- What were their own feelings with regard to him? They felt ashamed of the conduct of those lepers, but what should they say of themselves? They knew that Christ's life was lived and death died for their sins. Further they were there that night ostensibly to give God thanks for the mercies he had given them during the past year—there to acknowledge that all they *had had was from the guQd and gracious Lord. Bet was that their real thought ? If it were not God felt their ingratitude just as Christ felt the ingratitude of those men. But if they were ungrateful they were the greatest losers. God blessed the thankful heart. They were not told what became of the nine men who went away, hut the one leper who returned to give thanks had Christ's blessing, and God blessed those who were grate- ful and who recognised His goodness. The thankful spirit in them would be blessed, and they would be still richer spiritually and otherwise for the thankfulness shown. At that season of the year especially, let them thank God the giver of every good and perfect gift. Special hymns were rendered by the choir at each ser- vice, whilst the following antnems ..were sung: In the morning" 0 Lard how manifest ! " (Baraby), and in the evening " I will sing of Thy power" (Sullivan), the soloist being Mr. James Ditchfield. Mr. J. W. Jovvett presided at the organ, and had charge of the musical part of the services. The church had been tastefully decorated for the occasion by some of the ladies of the congregation, a special feature being an effectively piled display of fruit and vegetables in front of the pulpit- Satisfactory collections were obtained. Missionary Meeting at the Parr Hall. , The annual public meeting in connection with the Society for the Propagation of the Gospel in Foreign Parts was held on Tuesday evening in the Parr Hall. The chair was taken by the Lord Bishhp of Liverpool, who was supported on the platform by the Revs. Paul Bull (Airfield) and El. Moore (vicar of Acton and formerly missionary in Japan), the Rev. Canon Willis, the Rev. Canon Stevenson, the Rector of Grappenhall (the Rev. H. V. Pigot) and others.— The Bishop who was received with applause said, English people were put in trust with the Gospel. Why had God promoted them and made them one of the foremost among the nations of the earth? Why had He given to them an open Bible and a pure faith? He had them put in trust with the Gospel. To-day the English flag floated in every land. The British vessel ploughed the waters of every sea. The British trader or traveller pressed into every known country in the purstit of gain of pleasure. Wherever the British trader land traveller went there mist the British missionary Igo. Wherever the British flag floated there must the standard of the Cross be raised. Wherever the British tongue was heard there must the story of the Lord be told., Unless they fulfilled their mission, unless they carried out their trust, God was certain to remove their camtlestick from them as a church and His blessing from them as a people. Therefore,with the motives of obedience, love and faithfulness before them, they as English people and especially as English Churchpeople were bound to take an interest in missionary work and to help forward the day of which God's revealed Word spoke : " When the knowledge of the Lord shall cover the earth as the waters cover the sea." (Applause.) The Rev. Paul Bull delivered an interesting and char- acteristic address. The Church of this land depended. upon its missionary zeal and in sending its sons and daughters out to obey the Saviours great command. When the Church of England was sore pressed by her enemies, and was called upon to fight the most glorious battle that had ever yet fallen to her lot, to fight Christ's cause triumphantly back again into all their schools and to drive away the enemy who would banish definite Christian teaching from their children's life; at a time like this, when they were sore pressed at home,and yet assnred of certain victory in the long run, if only they had the courage and faith of their forefathers, now was the time to send their best abroad in order that smiting Satan's kingdom at another point might relieve the strain upon them in England, for the forces of God would win whether in England or in distant lands. (Applause.) It was an attack along the whole of the line by which they would be delivered here. (Applause.) —The Rev. H. Moore delivered a spirited address on Japan and its people, and took occasion to denounce the opium traffic to China and said Japan had set us an example that money was not the only thing to be con- sidered, as against the demoralizing of 350 millions of people when the Chinese Government were wanting to put the evil down and the English would not let them. —The collection amounted to £l2 7s.

The date fixed for the WHAT reassembling of Parliament THE LORDS is now so near that the PROPOSE, publication of the terms of

Lord AMPTHILL'S proposed amendments of the Education Bill is like a summons to battle. In Lord AlurraiLL's amend- ments may be recognised the proposals of the Established Church. There is a certain simple ingenuity in this putting forward of a lay peer to lay the train and fire the mine. Any Bishop who should undertake the task would be suspect. The country would see in the Bishop the Church Establishment fighting for its supremacy, and determined also, to use all possible means of gstting the children of Nonconformists into the clerical fold. It is expected to perceive something quite different when Lord AMPTHILL puts himself at the head of the Church forces. The Bishops always have presumed to a very large extent on the simplicity of their countrymen. In this instance the difference is merely between the tail wagging the dog and the dog wagging the tail. The relationship of the dog to the rest of the world remains unchanged in either case.

Lord AMPTIIILL'S amendments would be tanta- mount to the rejection of the Bill if they were adopted by the House of Lords. It would be wholly impossible for the Minister of Education to accept any material portion of them. They do not provide even a possible basis for arrangement. They would sweep much of the Government measure away, and they would so transform most of the remainder as to make it unrecognisable. It is proposed to deal with the religious difficulty by enlarging its proportions to enormous dimensions. The local authorities are to be compelled to give religion on three days a week in accordance with the wishes of the parents. The difficulty of stating how the parents' wishes are to ascertained is carefully avoided. No suggestion is made on that subject. What is provided for by the proposed atnendments is the turning of every school—for the placing of the provided schools on an equality in this respect seems to be contem- plated—into a field of theological warfare. Lord AmrrifiLL's amendments must, we suppose, be regarded as a manifesto not only of the Bishops, but of the vastly preponderating majority of the I House of Lords.ln these cases the Lords do what the Bishops tell them. Many of them,we have no doubt, act under a high sense of duty. A course of action may be inspired by the finer feelings, though it may be most lamentably misguided in itself. The Peers have been lately taught to believe that they are representative of the real feeling of the nation. Open to flattery of that sort, they have come to believe that they think and act as the people would think and act if they were in their right mind. Unfortunately, this is not a harmless delusion. It is exceedingly haralful. It finds us lighting year after year for mere acts of justice and good policy. Toe issue that is now to be raised is a decisive one, though the method adopted is a subterfuge. Whatever happens, there will not remain many illusions as to whether the mind of the country is best expressed by the House of ILords or by tbe people themselves.

Lord HUGH CECIL, we notice, has come forward with a proposal which, he thinks, will bring about a lasting peace with the Nonconformists. It is much the same as the proposal embodied in the Lords' amendments. There are to be ever so many sorts of religious teaching in every school, "to meet the wishes of the parents,"the parents having for the most part, expressed no wishes on the subject, and being heartily tired of the whole ecclesiastical wrangle. If Lord Huou CECIL wishes to make peace with the Nonconformists he should have thought of it earlier. What he was eager about when the Bill of 1892 was passing through the House of Commons was the conver- sion of that measure into a trap for Noncon- formist children. Any plan that he proposed, even a good one, would now be looked upon with suspicion ; but his actual plan is really a very bad one, and underlying it there seems to be an admis- sion that the Church cannot or will not do its duty to the young unless it be admitted to the class-rooms during school hours, with the conse- quence of a daily sorting of the sheep and the goats.
