Next Thursday, Mr. Have- VISIT OF MR. HAVE- lock Wilson, M.P., will visit LOCK WILSON, MX. Warrington in ider the auspices of the Progressive Labour League. It will be remembered that a week or two ago the Trades Council had an appeal before them on behalf of the Havelock Wilson Indemnity Fund, whicti they allowed to "lie on the table." Seeing the sort of persecution Mr. Wilson is being subjected to this was hardly coasiderate treatment from fellow representatives of Labour. Where the Trades Council failed, however, the Progressive Labour League has stepped in to champion Mr. Wilson's cause. They have secured him for a public meeting and are raising a local contribution to the indemnity fund. It is not too late for the Trades Council to follow their example and assist an object which should appeal closely to all trade unionists and labour men. It will be remembered that in 1900 the Shipping Federation obtained a verdict for £350 and costs against Mr. Havelock Wilson for libel contained in a leaflet issued in Glasgow durMg, the struggle of the Seamen's Uniou with the Federa- tion. 111 r. Wilson, the President of the Seamen's Union, was in London at the time the leaflet was issued, but he and another official had to bear the brunt of the action. When the judgment was obtained, Mr. Wilson ceased to be a member of Parliament, but was re-elected for Middlesborough this year. His advocacy of the cause ef the sea- men on the Merchant Shipping Bill and the Work- men's Compensation Bill seems to have stirred up the Federation again, and a demand has been made for the payment of £B4O, representing the damages and costs of the action in 1900. Mr. Wilson's attempts to settle the matter have been futile, and apparently the Shipping Federation is determined to force him out of Parliament. The workingmen of England can prevent this by sub- scribing to Mr. Wilson's assistance, and we hope the men of Warrington will not be behindhand in doing their share. Mr. Wilson speaki in the Co-operative Hall on Thursday evening next, and we hope he will be cheered in his fight by a rousing and sympathetic audience.

Stockton Heath is almost as TREE-PLANTING far off a settlement of the TROUBLES, tree planting difficulty as ever, and hard words were as plentiful as autumn leaves a.; the Parish Council meeting on Wednesday. It seems that the fiat is to go forth from the Runcorn Rural Council, following upon an inspection by the Highways Coro- mittee.As everyone expected, the committee has laid a heavy hand on some of the trees. All those on the north side of Whitefield•road are to be removed, and the same with every alternate tree on the south side, while stakes are ordered to be substituted for guards on the north side of Fairfield-road and elsewhere. But, more drastic still, the senior authority Ina; ruled that in future no trees be planted on foot- paths of a less width than seven feet. As the bye- laws only provide for a six-feet footpath this looks like an accidental—or intentional—prohibition of all future treeplanting.. The Parish Council is going to protest against the decision regarding Whitefield-road and the edict respe3ting a seven- feet footpath. There is reason in the contention that six-feet should be made the minimum, but with regard to the other rulings of the authority the Council will probably have to grin and obey. The tree-planters overdid things in their exuberant enthusiasm and opulence, and it was necessary that there should be some revision of the work they did not wisely but too well.

Meetings have been held LAND VALUES. in Warrington this week under the auspices of the English League for the Taxation of Land Talues, and have been addressed by Mr. Verinder the persuasive and able general secretary of the League. The audiences have not been large but they have been indicative of the growing earnestness of the public in their desire for a re-adjustment of social conditions, particularly those affecting the land question. Taxation of land values would not be plundering the landlords, but would be an act of simple justice to the people of the country. It would promote general prosperity and solve nearlY all the problems which beset the path of the social reformer. Unemployment would vanish—and John Burns is not unmindful of this fact—while rates and rents would probably come down by half. These are concrete facts which should aPPeal. to the humblest toiler who bears a man's bilities. He probably realises that the unimproved value of hind, which is created by the community and required for public use, is appro- priated by the owners of that land who have- done nothing to earn the increment. , If he can grasp this much then it should not be difficult for him to realise that his hope of social salvation lies with such an organisation as the League for the Taxation of Land Values. _Mem- bers of Parliament to the number of about 300 are actively concerned with the scheme and there is hope, even probability, of legislative action on progressive lines early next year. The prospect was never so bright as now, and the League hopes to attain during the lifetime of the present Parliament the objects—or a large proportion of them—for which it has been striving so many long and dreary years.

Organization is a TRADESMEN AND necessity of the age and the PROPERTY 0 %PEERS. tradesmen arid property owners of Warrington have realized the fact and have formed strong associa- tions to voice their claims. Both are in a very flourishing state and at the annual meetings held this week much useful work was recorded. Tno tradesmen have one great grievance, and that is the severe competition with which they have to con- tend from the markets. The local shop keepers have to meet their outside opponents on unequal terms, for the cost of a stand in the market is merely nominal compared with the burden of rents and rates which falls upon the local trades- men. The opposition of the Tradesmen's Associa- tion to a further extension of the market seerna perfectly reasonable and for the time being,at any rate, they have succeeded in carrying their views. The Property Owners' Association has been actively concerning itself with municipal affairs and there is reason to believe that its attitude on various vexed questions has had weight with the authorities. It was hardly necessary for the President to repudiate so warmly the suggestion that the organization has any party bias, for it is of a thoroughly representative char- acter, but perhaps the assurance will dispel any apprehensions which may have existed on the point. The Association takes an independent line on all questions affecting the progress of the town and, as the President explained, there is no desire to retard necessary reforms. Extravagance, how- ever, will be vigorously opposed and the efforts of the Association directed to securing efficiency with economy.

One does not try to master ELECTION the intricacies of American LANGUAGE. State pllitics. They are beyond the grasp of the ordinary individual in this country. But there is one aspect of them which is entertaining enough, and needs not to be deeply studied. Politics here are tame and unexciting compared with the New York brand. The New Yorker delights in finding good hard names for his opponents and hurling them at their heads. Mr. Hearst who is standing for the Governorship of New York owns an even- ing paper, the "Journal." This is how the "Journal "refers to Mr. Jerome, who is opposing Mr. Hearst: "This man with the shaking hands, the uncertain, inflamed mind the almost lunatic conceit and arrogance." Mr. Jerome does not however, take this lying down, for he retaliates by calling Mr. Hearst " intellectually sterile, socially vulgar, and morally obtuse '—a sufficiently sweeping indictment, one would think. To a Democratic editor in Louisville Mr. Hearst is beyond the reach of corruption, and may re relied upon to "cleanse the Augean stable. But to a Republican paper in the same city he appears as "a weak, unscrupulous, vain, and ambitious man, spotted child of great wealth,bred to the notion that his money could buy him any-

Theoretically, we are sup- BAGGAGE posed to be a Free Trade INSPECTION. countr y. Commercially speaking, the foreigner finds that we are. Nevertheless, it is a strange paradox that there is more fuss made by custom-house Officials with personal baggage at ports of entry to this country than is the case in tariff-protected uronean states such as France or Belgium. At Calais, Dieppe, Boulogne, Fushing or Ostend, baggage inspection, where a passenger makes no fuss and answers the questions put to him with apparent truthfulness is over. In our Free Trade ports such as aiver, iewhaven, Folkestone, or Harwich, holiday- makers are often unnecessarilSi worried by having trunks spread out to public gaze under the sus- ion that soiled linen may secrete packets of utiable tobacco, lace, eau-de-uolog,ne, or, possibly, a SOHO wine-cellar. The public generally is little EUTRALS IN WAR. interested now in the problems which were raised during the Russo-Japanese war as to extent to 'hick a belligerent is entitled to interfere with lipping on the high seas. But it may be well to draw some attention to the proceedings at the International Law Conference at Berlin dealing With this subject. Mr. J. E. R.Stephens,who read °iPsper on the Right of Search, pointed out that iugland herself acted during the seventeenth and a —ng',,teetith centuries with considerable inconsist- ecy• indeed, it is the fact that i „,--t 'as the uncertainty of the British position Which rendered the law relating to search tOn the high seas so unsatisfactory. Mr. -tePhons, speaking with the experience of the l'ar-Eastern conflict in mind, suggests a new set Of rules for international acceptance. He would have every neutral veßsel carry a certificate of neutrality signed by the Consul or some other Official. The belligerent should be limited to a right to visit a ship to ascertain her nationality, but should have no right of search. If a vessel were reasonably suspecte.d of containing contraband of War the belligerent should, however, have the r:ght of putting a prize-crew on board to convey the neutral to her next port of call, where if she fails to prove her neutrality she should be handed Over to the belligerent as a lawful prize. The geteral principle of these suggestions is good, and they would afford a protection to neutrals of which they stand greatly la need. NOTES AND NOTIONS.

We notice that the President of the Warrington Tradesmen's Association, Mr. W. Roberts, J.P., in the course of a speech on Tuesday at the annual meeting of this useful body, alluded to the division of opinion in the town on of the question of Corporation property purchases. While he thought that a considerable number of the purchases had been to the advantage of the Borough, Mr. Roberts emphasised —we are glad to note—the view that when the Property Purchasing Sub-committee had made a provisional agreement to purchase they should, as soon as possible, take the General Purposes Committee, which consists of the whole of the Council, into their confidence. The Non- Traders have long pressed for some arrangement of this kind, and have at last obtained the safeguard they require. It is satisfactory to find their attitude upheld by the President of an organisation so closely concerned with the interests of the town as the Tradesmen's Association. * * * It is dangerous sometimes for a journalist to write in parables. For instance, the London correspondent of a Manchester paper sent the other day a humorous account of the renewing of the hands of the great clock of Parliament. It. ran thus —" A well-known character, who has lived in Westminster all his life, and is familiarly known to many Londoners as Ben,' underwent a serious operation this morning. As Westminster Hospital was too full to allow of him having a bed, the operation was carried out by two doctors in the open air, and in the presence of a large crowd. Both of Ben's hands were taken off —successfully and rapidly. A new pair will be provided for him." The news editor puts this item in a prominent position among the day's painful occurrences, next to a paragraph headed " Maniac Kills Seven," and gave it the following headline : " Operation in the Open Air —Big London Crowd Watched Man's Hands Taken Oft"

EDUCATION COMMITTEE. Business of the —Next Meeting. REPORTS ON TEACHERS' WORK.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Education Committee will be held at the Town Hall at six o'clock on Monday evening when the business for consideration will include the following :

THE BEA-INIONT SCHOOL. The Finance Committee resolved that application be made to the Board of Education for their sanction to the charge of the following fees at the new "Beamont Council school, viz. :—in the Infants' department ld per week, in the Boys' and Girls' departments 2d. per week ; 90 free places being retained in each department, i those n the senior department being distributed chiefly among the upper classes. "OVERWORK AND BID VENTILATION."

At a meeting of the School Management Committee a letter from the managers of the Trinity National school—recommending that leave of absence for three months be granted to the Head Mistress of the Infants' department of that school—was submitted together with a medical certificate to the effect that this teacher's illness is due to overwork and to working in badly ventilated rooms. It was resolved : That leave of absence for three months, from the 20th September, 1906, be granted to Miss Dorothy Bee, Head Mistress of the Infants' department of the Trinity National school, on account of ill-health, upon the customary conditions, viz.:—The payment of full salary during the first, half salary during the second, and without salary during the third month of absence. At a subsequent meeting a letter from the managers of the Trinity National school—in explanation of the steps taken by them to improve the ventilation of the Infants' department of that school—was submitted and noted. LF.CTURES Oti ALCOHOL. An application from the Lancashire and Cheshire Band of Hope and Temperance Union, for permission to give lessons at certain schools in the Borough on the use of alcohol, was granted.

===== 0002601_19061013_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
REPORTS ON TEACEiERS' WORK.

It havil7lll;e7l-1:4;o-ite-d—th;i---tiFe; resolution of the committee at their last meeting in reference to the half-yearly reports upon assistant teachers had not been approved and adopted by the Education Com- mittee, it was, after some discussion, decided to re- affirm the resolution, and it was accordingly resolved that, in future, the reports upon the conduct and work af the assistant teachers be only required from the head teachers and managers when the same are of an unsatisfactory character ; and that, when such a report is received, the teacher so reported upon shall be notified of the fact,and shall be afforded an opportunity of stating his or her case before the committee. SWIMMING INSTRUCTION. ' '

In reference to the report by the keeper of the baths upon the teaching of swimming, the Director reported that H. M. Inspector had intimated his willingness to sanction the attendance of the scholars at the baths during school hours and it was resolved that the Com- mittee agree to the times for the attendance of the scholars the baths suggested in the report; and that the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee be approached with a view to their sanction being obtained to the proposed arrangements, and to the services of the keeper of the baths, as instructor, being obtained.

in view of the iernii-fWie'il.:4;;;-the'2-lss.t- October, 1903, of the existing arrangements with tha Gymnasium Com- mittee, in connection with the teaching of physical exercises, it was resolved, that the Gymnasium Com- mittee be approached with a view to ascertaining upon what terms they would be prepared (a) to provide a class at the Gymnasium for the instruction of teachers who have not got a certificate or qualification for the teaching of physical exercises ; and (b) to allow the use of the Gymnasium to enable the scholars of the Secondary school to receive instruction in physical exercises from the teachers of that school during the year commencing on the Ist November, 190 G. CAPTAIN CROSFIELD'S REQUEST. A letter was read from Captain George Crosfield, asking to be allowed to draw the attention of the boys of the Secondary school to the suggested provision of facilities for the practise of gun shooting, and the Director was requested to intimate, in reply, that the Committee could not see their way to accede to his request,but that they had no objection to his approach- ing the boys upon the subject by circular letter or by arranging a meeting with them elsewhere than at the school, outside of school hours should he desire to do so.

PROPOSED NEW SCHOOL. It was resolved that the Law Clerk be instructed to give the public notice required by Section 8 of the Education Act, 1902, of the committee's intention to erect a public elementary school to accommodate 630 mixed scholars and 369 infants in the neighbourhood of Wellfield-street ; and that application be made to the Board of Education for the continued recognition of the infants' department of the Trinity National school pending the propision of this accommodation. P'AYMENT OF LABOURERS. The replies to the enquiries as to the rate of payment to labourers actually supplying bricklayers by various methods, were submitted, and it was resolved that the Secretary of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council and the contractor for the erection of the Beamont Council school be informed that the Committee are satisfied that the labourers supplying bricklayers at that schbol are being paid in accordance with the terms of the contract.

BUSSEYS' SPORTS REQUISITES. Messrs. a G. Bussey and Co.. Ltd., the great sports outfitters and manufacturers, of Peckham, have issued two very interesting illustrated pamphlets which should be welcomed by all lovers of winter sports. One of these is about hockey stick making and shows by text and illustration the production of a high-class hockey stick from the ash-tree to the finished article. Designs and prices follow in such profusion that the taste of everyone should be suited. One of the tips the firm gives is " don't have your stick polished." If so it is impossible to oil it effectually. Another interesting little pamphlet is about Busseys' footballs, which are shown in all qualities and prices. In some introduc- tory chat about footballs, Messrs. Bussey point out that a square foot of leather may be worth 6d. or 65., and either piece be correctly termed leather. One pound of India rubber may be worth is. or 125., and still come under the same title. Obviously, therefore, it pays to get your goods from a firm with a reputation worth maintaining, and who can be relied upon to put the best material in everything they produce. Messrs. Bussey recommend customers to go to the local sports dealer, who will supply catalogues and forward their orders.

ST. HELENS ROMAN CATHOLIC BAZAAR. A four-days' bazaar in aid of the building fund of St Vincent's Roman Catholic Church and schools, Derby- shire-hill, was opened at St. Helens Town Hall on Wednesday by Lady Gerard, who made her first public appearance in the district after her marriage. The mission, which is in charge of Father James O'Connell, formerly of St. Joseph's, Liverpool, is in debt to the extent of £2,560, and it is hoped that over £l,OOO will be raised this week. Messrs. J. A. Collins, W. Bromilow, and J. Frodsham are the treasurers, and Messrs. Joseph Sephton and A. A. Slater secretaries.— Lady Gerard, on I entering the assembly-room, was presented with a beautiful bouquet of roses by Miss Maggie Riley, one lof the school children.—Dean Powell, of Birchley, presided, and in introducing Lady Gerard said there I were nine churches or chapels in St. Helens. Three of them had distriots in which there were 21,000 Catholics, three of moderate size bad 6,000, and three smaller ones had 3,000. so that in St. Helens they had 30,50 Catholics—not one less, whatever - )ple might say .and

peep._ _ however people might vote. St. Helens was one of the most Catholic towns in all England.—Lady Gerard was very warmly received, an said she was delighted to come to St. Helens and help. She declared the bazaar open, and hoped that the public would be generous in their purchases. A vote of thanks was carried with acclamation, on the motion of the Rev. Father Hearne, seconded by Dr. Bennett.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON

BURTON WOOD NEW WESLEYAN CHAPEL. The opening services in connection with the Button- wood new Wesleyan Chapel were continued on Sunday when the preacher was the Rev. T. Turnbull, of Dids- bury. In the afternoon a musical service was rendered by the Earlestown Wesleyan choir and friends. SINGULAR ACCIDENT TO A SCHOOLBOY. A singular accident befel a boy _named Leyland, of 53, King-street, Earlestown, on Tuesday morning. He was playing in the Wesleyan School ground, Earles- town when he and another boy bumped their heads together, and Leyland fell to the ground. On being medically attended it was found that he was suffering from concussion of the brain. DRUNKENNESS. At the Earlestown Police Court on Monday, before Mr. T. Last week at the Warrington County Petty Sessions, Herbert Mawdsley, of Earlestown, applied for a certifi- cate exempting his child from vaccination. He said he had a conscientious objection to vaccination because he believed it would Is harmful to the health of the child. The Chairman (Captain Withiugton) : That is quite sufficient. We are obliged to grant it, although we do so very unwillingly. FARMER AND BAKER AT LAW. At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, befoie his Honour Judge Shiress Will, K.C., an action WAS I heard in which Richard Alderson, farmer, Newton, claimed £3 10s. from Christopher Holt, baker, Earles- town, for damage alleged to have beau caused to crops by the defendant's hens. There was a counter-claim against Alderson for £5 for allowing his horses and cows to stray in Holt's field.—Mr. H. J. Longtou appeared on behalf of Alderson, and Mr. C. H. Unsworth I represented Holt.—After an exhaustive hearing his Honour gave judgment for plaintiff on the claire for I the amount claimed with costs and on the counter-claim he awarded Holt 10s. with costs. HARVEST FESTIVALS.

RAGGED SCHOOL AND MISSION.

IThe harvest festival services in connection with the ' Ragged Sunday School and Mission, Bank-street, were I held on Sunday atternoon and evening, the preacher I being Mr. Drewett. Special hymns were sung by the choir and friends under the conductorshop of Mr. F. Owen. Mr. falakemore presided at the organ. A large I number of friends attended, and the collections which I were very satisfactory were in aid of the Sunday school. The room was prettily decdrated with corn, fruit, flowers, vegetables, &c., and the work reflected great credit upon all concerned. HILL CLIFFE BAPTIST CHURCH.

Harvest thanksgiving services were held in the above , church on Sunday, when the building was beautifully J decorated for the occasion with choice floNers and corn. Gifts of fruit and vegetables were also numerous. The preacher for the day was the Rev. Lewis M. Thomas, Openshaw, Manchester. Special hymns were sung, the choir rendering the anthem," 0, Lord, how ! manifold." Mr. jGaudy presided at the organ. The j congregations were large. On Monday night a " fruit I social" was held in the schoolroom, which was also ! decorated for the occasion, and a good number of , friends met for social intercourse, and after enjoying several games, the fruit and vegetables were sold.

TABLE FLOOR, AND HANGING LAMPS in lame variety. leitest patterns.—J. S. GARLAND, Iron- monger, Bridge st., Warrington.—Advt. Y" Branch B.W.T.A.

_ The opening members' meeting of the winter session was held in the Wihmarlegh Cafe (the new head quarters of the society) on Wednesday evening. A Mrs. Milne was unfortunately prevented from attend- ing through ill-health, Miss Dutton presided. In her address she dwelt on the great use of individual work, and told the girls not to be downcast at the lack of response but to be sure that some good was done by theil• efforts. After a song from Miss Jackson, Miss May Isherwood, hon. sec., then read her report of the annual conference held in London, to which she went as delegate. It was a very interesting account, particularly in its detailing of the lectures given by various presidents. Among the arrange- ments for the winter it was decided that some of the members should visit the girls' club at Sliver-street, once a week, Tuesday being the night chosen.

Church Clubs' Billiard League.

Games in the above league commence next, Thursday as follows :-Ist division : St. Paul's v. St. Clement's, Padgate v. St. George's 2nd division : St. Clement's v. St. Paul's, St. George's v. Padgate. To be played on the table of the first-named club. The members of the respective clubs are busy practising to meet their engagements in the tournament, and all round, great interest is being taken in the League. The League was only formed last season as an experiment, but the success was so marked, that it may now be looked upon as a permanency. The outlook for the forth- coming winter is especially rosy and great enthusiasm existing among the players portends to many keen struggles for supremacy. Results of matches and tables will appear in these columns from time to time. • WOOD CHIMNEY PIECES, Fire Grates and Tiles, unrivalled selection, inspection invited.—J. S. GARLAND, Builders' Ironmonger, Sm., 51 to 55, 1 Bridge st., Warrington.—Advt. ' Labour Bureau. _

-- --- There have been registered during the week 40 persons, making the total number now registered 1,035. During the week 45 have obtained woik, and there are now on the register out of employment 187 persons. The following is a list of the different trades :—General labourers, 35; wire trade labourers, 6 ; iron trade labourers, 21 ; builders' labourers, 4 ; bricklayers, 4; joiners, 3; fitters and pipefitters, 5; clerks and warehousemen, 14 ; engine drivers and firemen, 7 ; carters and van men, 12 ; grocers assistant, 1 ; girls (under 18), 3 ; youths (under 18), 16 ; charwomen, 28; men of 50 years old and upwards, 29. During the week several ladies have made inquiry at the bureau for domestic servants, and there are now several superior situations regis- tered and waiting for suitable maids to fill them. All who have obtained work should immediately inform the Clerk at the bureau of their having done so, otherwise it will be impossible to keep a correct record of persons out of work.

Co-00erat1ve Society's Lecture, Under the auspices of the Educational Committee of the Warrington Co-operative Society, a lecture was given in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street. ou Thursday, by Mr. Henry Hibbert, (member of the London Polytechnic). The subject was "Beauties of the Emerald Isle," illustrated by photo- graphs and animated pictures specially produced for this lecture. The chair was occupied by Mr. Thomas Critchley, president of the Society. Admission was free both to members and non-members. There was a good attendance. This was the Lecturer's first visit to Warrington, but he fully sustained the reputation he holds throughout the country as a lecture of the highest standing. He has travelled largely, and this enables him to give most instructive, enlightening, and entertaining word pictures. His photographs of Ireland were of the highest order, and won the admira- tion and applause of the audience.. Jie has been fortunate enough in his photographic journeys to some of the remote parts in Ireland to have met Lord and Lady Dudley, and been asked to accompany them and a party which included the King and Queen, and to have obtained unique photographs and animated pictures of these eminent personages, their receptiOns, and their visits to the poor people. The Educational Committee have arranged for a winter series of lectures, concerts, &c., which we feel sure will be greatly appreciated by the members of the Society.

THE NOVEMBER ELECTIONS. Nomination Day.

Wednesday, October 24th, is the last day for the receipt of nominations of candidates at the municipal elections on November Ist. There have been several fresh developments in the situation during the past week. Mr. T. S. Steel has acceded to a request of the Conservatives to oppose Councillor Maginn in St. John's Ward but we hear it is doubtful whether Mr. E. Plinston will coma forward in Latchford Ward. Mr William McNeil Sloane has definitely decided to stand in the Conservative interest in Orford Ward where his oppo- nents will be Councillor Cunnington (Non-Trader) and Mr. William Pook (rJabour). WARRINGTON CONSERVATIVE ASSOCIATION. ._ New Officers Appointed.

The adjourned general annual meeting of the War- rington Conservative Association was held on Monday evening in the Assembly Room of the Conservative Club. Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., (president) occupied the chair, and there was a large attendance of members.

THE LATE ALDERMAN ROBERTS. The PRESIDENT moved a resolution regretting the death of the late Alderman Roberts.—Alderman FRAN- COMB seconded, and the motion was passed in silence. MR. JOHN WHITE'S SERVICES. Councillor FULTON SMITH proposed that the best thanks of the Association be given to Mr. John White for the valuable services he had rendered as Chairman during the past 21 years. He paid a high tribute to the work of Mr. White on behalf of the Party. Mr. W. L. WILLIAMS seconded the resolution, and Sir Gilbert Greenall spoke in support of it. The resolution of thanks was very heartily passer. Mr. WHITE, who had a hearty reception, said he was not going into obscurity, He hoped to be able to be of some assistance still to the party, and wowld certainly render all the assistance he could to Mr. Rylands, his successor. (Applause.) They might depend upon him, concluded Mr. White, so long as life and health were left him, to support the Conservative cause to the best of his abilty.

OFFICERS. The following elections were made :—President, Sir Gilbert Greenell, Bart. ; deputy-presidents, Messrs. J. Charlton Parr, R. Pierpoint and John White ; vice- presidents, subscribers of £5 and upwards ; chairman, Mr. W. Peter Rylands ; deputy-chairman, Councillor J. Fulton Smith ; hon. secr•etary, Mr. E. J. Hall ; hon. treasurer, Mr. Segar Owen ; hon. legal adviser, Mr. E. Draper.

DEATH OF WARRINGTON'S OLDEST INHABITANT.

Warrington's oldest inhabitant passed away on Thurs- day afternoon in the person of Hr. James -Marsh, 22, Rolleston-street. The deceasod gentleman, who would , have been ninety-eight years of age next month, had I been confined to his bed for the past three weeks and had been attended by Dr. Smith. He was conscious almost to the last. All his life deceased had resided in Warrington or the vicimity, having bean born at Nook Houses, Padgate. For forty-seven years he was a fore- man platelayer on the London and North-Western Railway but retired between thirty and forty years ago. He had many interesting recollections of War- rington in the old days and could recall the time when the stage coaches ran. He had quite vivid remem- brances of the celebrations of the victory at Waterloo and he attended a banquet held at Padgate Stocks in , honour of the Coronation of King George IV. He had always taken the greatest interest in the development and progress of the town and its industries. He was a Churchman, and worshipped at St. Paul's Church. He was also a staunch Conservative and was Warrington's oldest voter, recording his vote at the last General I Election. By the members of the Ancient Order of I Shepherds, of which he was an active member, and by a very large circle of townspeople he was held in the deepest respect. Of his eighteen children, eight (five sons and three daughters) are living, as well as forty- eight grandchildren and ninety-three great-grandchil- -1 dren. The funeral will take place at the Cemetery at I 1.45 on Sunday afternoon.

Aidebtor at Bow County Court, on Wednesday, said he was quite unable to pay. The solicitor asked, "What are your means ?" The debtor replied : "I am sure I don't know. I only came'out of Brixton Prison after 21 day for debt last night, and, coming here this morn- ing, I haven't had time to inquire."

" lOLA NTHE."

NEXT WEEK'S PERFORMANCES AT WARRINGTON. Mr. F. H. Crossley and the Musical Promise of the Opera.

Warrington people are looking forward with con- siderable interest and pleasure to the performances of " lolanthe " which are t be given in the Parr Hall every evening nest week by members of the Musical Society. This is the fourth amateur operatic enter- prise which the Society has engaged in and we believe there is every reason to predict that it will be highly successful. The rehearsals have been entered into with the greatest enthusiasm and musically and dramatic- ally, as well as from the spectacular point of view, the production promises to worthily maintain the repu- tation the opera contingent of the Society has attained. The decision of the Opera Committiee to devote a pro- portion of the proceeds to the Infirmary extension fund will, we are sure, commend itself to the towns- people, who will no doubt be glad to avail themselves 'of this opportunity of helping forward a most deserving the object of gleaning some information as to PthreoWjmecith uts. ical promise of " lolanthe " a representative of "The Examiner" interviewed Mr. Frank H. Crossley, the able conductor of the Society, who has directed the rehearsals and worked indefatigably to ensure a creditable production of the work in hand. Speaking of the opera itself, Mr. Crossley said it was one of the most 'beautiful from the spectacular point of view as well as one of the most popular of the Gilbert and Sullivan collabora- tions. It did not contain quite as much of the comic element as " The Mikado" but where it scored particu- larly was in its daintiness. The subject was fairy- land and the whole setting of the opera was char- acterised by exceeding brightness and gracefulness. The music was, however, decidedly strenuous in parts —as, for instance, the March of the Peers which

_ - - -- forms, so conspicuo,us a pageant in the first act—while on the other hand there were more light touches than were to be found in most other of the Gilbert and - „.

Sullivan operas.

Mr. Crossley then entered into details with regard to " forces " engaged in next week's performances. We have a slightly larger and more powerful chortle he said "than we have had before and I think

it may safely be said that it is a better chorus all round."

Alluding to the work done at the rehearsals, Mr. Crossley said he had felt very pleased with the splendid enthusiasm and the loyalty of all the members. The rehearsals began early in May and had continued through the summer with a month's vacation in

A.ugu—st. Very few people outside the Society could have any idea of the amount of work required to bring a production of this kind to the necessary degree of efficiency and completeness. He had personally attended thirty-five evening rehearsals. The principals, continued Mr. Crossley, had, with two exceptions, all appeared in previous operas, and lie believed the new comers might be expected to uphold the standard attained in the opera committee's three previous ventures. Mr. Crossley laid stress upon the absolutely local character of the gproduction. It. I was, he said, one of the conditions that everyone who appeared on the stage, whether a principal or a member of the chorus, should be a member of the Musical Society. With regard to the orchestra, it was a matter for pride and gratification that it should consist entirely of local performers. "There are no doubt " he remarked " many other very excellent operatic societies in the kingdom but I feel quite sure there is not one that gives performances of anything like the degree of completeness that we do, while relying upon an entirely local amateur orchestra. It is largely on that account that the financial results are so satisfactory. A professional orchestra of the size of our's would cost at least :£.lOO a week."

" Then you are confident as to the success of the performances ?" asked the interviewer. " Yes " said Mr. Crossley. " Barring accidents, I think they will be as successful as any we have had." As showing the resources of the Society, Mr. Cross- ley pointed out that all the principal parts were under- studied. This is the first time that such a c.nirse has been taken. The Society had been very fortunate hitherto in always having the services of the selected ptincipals at the performances, but recognizing the risk there is of anything untoward happening at the % last _moment, it was decided to have under-studies.

The attendances at all the rehearsals, said Mr. Crossley, had been splendid.

===== 0002601_19061013_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
A CHAT Win MR, THORN LEY DODGE. THE DRAMATIC AND SPECTACULAR SIDE OF "lOLANTHE."

The Opera Committee is to be congratulated upon again securing the services of Mr. E. Thornley-Dodge as dramatic instructor and stage manager. He has had considerable experience in operatic productions, and much of the credit for the success of the Society's previous enterprises is due to him. Interviewed by our representative, Mr. Dodge gave some interesting opinions as to next week's performances of "-lolanthe." Dealing particularly with the dramatic and spectacular side, he expressed his entire satisfac- tion with the progress made at the rehearsals, and his confidence in the capability of the company. " Every- one " he said " seems to have anticipated what I wanted and to have done it well." Mr. Dodge spoke in terms of the warmest praise of the company generally, and said it contained talent of a high order. Vocally, he thought it was better than ever and he believed the new corners would quite realize expectations. "I suppose the spectaeular effects will be a great

eattire?

"Yes " replied Mr. Dadge. "I hope when the curtain rises on the first act that the people of War- rington will say they have never seen such a beautiful stage setting before." Alludino, to tha opara,Mr.Dodge pointed out that the music and action of" lolanthe were of an exception- ally light and sparkling character. The librettist had, however, introduced quite a daring touch of seriousness towards the close. The Chancellor, after playing comedy throughout, had to enter almost abruptly upon a dramatic scene, but he thought that Mr. C. P. Gough, who is entrusted with the rote,would be quite equal to this. Mr. Dodge voiced the pleasure which we are sure the townspeople will feel that Miss Mabel Henshall will again figure in one of the leading parts. Her performance, he was convinced, would prove her ability to excel in a totally different character from any which she has previously undertaken. She has the one dramatic song o: the opera - certainly one of its gems,—to interpret. A curious circumstance which Mr. Dodge mentioned is that Ors lover in " lolanthe "is not a tenor as is generally the case. There is, he believes, every probability that the baritone who is announced to take the part will do so with distinction. Two of the leading gentlemen in the cast will appear in roles quite unlike any they have hitherto filled and this feet will no doubt arouse additional interest in the performances. We understand that Mr. Thornley Dodge has already been responsible for the " coaching " of two operatic societies—Brooklands and St. Annes-on-Sea—in " lolanthe " and in each production he himself took the part of the Chancellor.

Fishing Match. The last of the series of matches in connection with the King Edward VII. Coronation Club of Jolly Anglers was finished at Thelwall on Saturday after- noon in beautiful weather. Andrews, Bellairs, Donalchion, J. Boscow, S. Foulkes, and C. Mather. UNDER THE CLOCK for Good BOOTS. Advt.— Coal Merchant's Suicide. An exceedingly sad case of suicide was investigated at Warrington on Wednesday evening when Mr. S. Brighouse, County Corouer, held an inquest an Olbin Emmess, coal merchant, 3, Roman-road, Stockton Heath. Mary Emmess, deceased's wife, said that for the past three weeks her husband had complained of being unwell but would not have medical aid. He had been very much depressed owing to the death of his favourite brother. One day last week he told witness that he wished he was dead.—Albert Buckley, a carter employed by the deceased, stated that after going with witness to a coal yard in Gee.street on Tuesday morning, deceased told him to take the horse and lurry to Parr-street. Witness did so and afterwards went to the stable in the Norton Arms hotel yard where he found deceased hanging by a rope attached to a beam. Witness called for the ostler who came and cut the deceased down. Dr. Smith was sent for but life was extinct. Deceased had complained recently about his back being bad and he had been unable to lift anything.—A. verdict of " Suicide whilst of unsound mind "was returned. Junior 'Liberal Association. There was a good attendance at the weekly meeting of the Warrington Junior Liberal Association on Monday evening when the proceedings took the nature of a debate on Mr. Birrell's Edecation Bill. The lead in the affirmative was given by Mr. H.W. Guest. It was, he said, entirely impracticable for the children during certain school hours to receive instruction in accordance with the religions beliefs of their parents. Not only was it impractical but to attempt it would be to increase the feeling between the different Christian churches. Mr. Guest car9fully reviewed the history of education in England which had created the necessity for the introduction of the Bill. The only solution of the difficulty lay in a compromise and be thought that as a compromise the Bill would be satisfactory ta the nation as a whole. (Hear, hear.) —lt was nnfortunate that the gentleman who bad been deputed to lead the opposition was unable to be present but his place was taken by Mr. H. Gray who, despite the shortness of the notice, succeeded in making out a good case. —ln the, debate which I followed, the general feeling expressed favoured Secular education. Farm Labourer's Shocking Death. A farm labourer named James Burke (23),employed by Alfred Cook, Cherry Tres farm, Cow-lane, Sankey, met with a shockinr, death on Wednesday. The circumstances were re'lated at the inquest which was conducted by Mr. S. Brighouse the same evening.— Mr Qook stated that unknown to him deceased went with a horse and cart to fetch a load of cinders from the Mersey White Lead works. witness went to Warrington with a lurry-load of potatoes and on returning he was overtaken in Cow-lane by a butcher's cart. The vehicle passed him but shortly returned , and the driver told witness that something was wrong I and asked him to jump into the cart. He did so 1 and dris)ing a little further down the lane they found the horse and cart which deceased had taken out standing still and deceased ling in the roadway , in a shocking state. The near cart wheel had passed over his head. Deceased was immediately conveyed to the Infirmary but was dead on arrival there.—PC. Wood said the doctor at the Infirmary stated that deceased's brain was crushed.—lt was also stated that deceased had been seen riding on the shafts and it is presumed that he fell off and sustainnd the terrible injuries which resulted fatally.—A verdict of "Accidental death" was returned. , PUBLIC BF.NEFIT, UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt. , I Massed Band Performanne. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

STREET TRADING.—WARNING TO NEWSBOYS. On Monday, at the Borough Police Court, a number of newsboys were summoned for neglecting to wear their badges, trading without a licence, or trading after eight o'clock in the evening, on October 3rd. The offenders were :—John Thynne (12), 62, Back Dallam- lane ; Samuel Dickenson (12), 28, Foundry-street ; James Thompson (3), 2, Dial-court ; Thomas Burns (13), 4, Stanley-street ; Thomas Hickey(l2), 4, Brown's. yard ; and James Nunnerley (11), 42, Upper James-st. —The Chief Constable explained that some three years ago the Watch Corn.mittee deemed it necessary to frame certain bylaws to fegulate street trading, and in doing so they incurred and were still incurring considerable expense and trouble. There was an understanding between the Watch Committee and himself that so far as possible he should avoid prosecutions in this matter, and up to now he had abided by that undertaking, having dealt with offending boys by either cautioning, or anspeuding, or, in extraordinary cases, doing away with the licence. The time had arrived, however, when he could no longer take such a responsibility. Leniency seemed to be a mistake. The Association of News. venders of the town, many of whom were large rate- payers, had petitioned the Watch Committee, and when he made investigations he found they had strong cause to complain of these newsboys standing in front of their shops calling their wares, and without wearing their badges, so that they could not be identified. The boys also went into the public-houses,and, in fact, broke every single regulation set forth on the cards supplied to them. They had set everything at defla.nce.—The boys were lined Is. each, and the gayor warned them that if they repeated the offence they would be locked up.

===== 0002601_19061013_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
ALLEGED HOLTSEBREAKING AT WARRINGTON.

At the Warrington Police Court, on Tuesday, James Towey, a ticket-of-leave man, was charged with burglariously, entering the dwelling-house of Richard Milner, 255, Winwick-road, at midnight on Saturday, alnd stealing various articles of clothing, and its. sd. in cash. It was stated that Mrs. Milner securely fastened the windows and doors on Saturday night before retir- ing to bed. The following morning the scullery window was found broken open and several articles of clothing missing. In consequence of a communication a visit was paid to the prisoner's lodgings by Mrs. Milner, who found him wearing a jacket and vest belonging to her son. A sum of money amounting to 16s. 3d. had been taken from the pockets of Mr. Milner whilst he was asleep. Prisoner at first denied all knowledge of the theft, but when confronted with the husband he , delivered up the cash and clothing.—Samuel Milner, a thirteen-year-old son, a member of the Warrington Ragged School Flute Band, on returning from Notting- ham at 1.30 on Sunday morning, called at a companion's house, where the prisoner lodged, and there was asked by prisoner to play a flute which he recognised as his own instrument.—The magistrates committed Towey to the Liverpool Quarter Sessions.

HAWKERS' THEFT.

At Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday, Edward Birchall and Thomas Birchall, hawkers, Warrington, were charged with stealing reins, an electric battery and a horse cloth, of the value of £1 63. 6d., the property of the Warrington Consolidated Oil Com- pany.—Mr. Steel appeared for Edward Birchall.—Carter Machell said he was delivering oil at Stretton on the 27th September, and had occasion to leave the cart for a few minutes. When he returned he missed the reins, the battery and cloth.—lnformation was given to the police.—P.C. Andrew, of the Warrington Police Force, said he was at the Police Station on the evening of the 27th September, when he was informed of the robbery. In consequence of certain information received he pro- ceeded to Wilderspool-road, where he saw the defend- ants in charge of a hawker's cart. H eand P.C. Taylor went to the cart and found the reins.—The case against Edward was dismissed; Thomas was fined 20s. and costs.

ROBBERY FROM MESSRS. BELLIAN AND D.A.VIES. - MONEY IN THE 11F.LLOWS.

At the Warrington Police Court on Wednesday,Alf red Boswell, (16), Wakefleld-street, and Sidney Blease (IS), Norman-street, were charged with stealing £33 from the shop of Messrs. Bellian and Davies, motor and cycle manufacturers, Sankey-street, between Saturday night and Monday morning, and Edwin Hamar, a married man residing in Longford-street, was charged with receiving the money. They all pleaded guilty.—The Chief Constable stated that Boswell had been employed by Bellian and Davies for about two years, and that in company with Blease he entered the shop by the back door, which he opened with a duplicate key. He then found a bunch of keys in the shop, opened the till drawer, and the two took the money. Suspicion fell on Boswell, and when challenged, he admitted taking the keys of the shop home, but added : "I did not steal anything." Blease also denied all knowledge of the affair, but when the two men were brought face to face in the police station they owned to the theft. They said a man named Williams in Longford-street had the money. —Detective-Sargeant Meacock and Detective Clough failed to find any Williams, but discovered Hamar, who stoutly denied he had the money. He was, however, arrested, and £26 was found in a pair of bellows. The lest of the money had been squandered.—Boswell now said Blease took the money ; Blease put the blame on Boswell ; and Hamar said Sloes° asked him to take care of the money for a week.—The Bench sentenced Boswell to a week's Imprisonment in the second division, and remarked that they were of opinion he had got into bad company. Blease and Hamar got two months' hard labour each.—The detectives were complimented on their smart work in connection with the case.

AN INTEkESTING CENTENARY.

STOCKTON HEATH, 1806-1906

[BY ARTHUR MOM, FIELD.]

Late in December, 1805, a small sailing vessel lay at anchor in the estuary of the Mersey. She had taken up her passengers at New York on November 10tb, and favourable winds had enabled her to cast anchor in the estuary on December Bth. The delays which beset the voyager of a century ago were numerous. One of the passengers wrote in his journal:— The ship carpenters came and examined our rudder; lnd made reports accordingly to the officers of Government relative to our state. We were exempted from quarantine after a detention of ten days (which time passed heavily away two miles above the town in the river), as we had a bill of health from the British Consul.

If it took ship with a clean bill of health, one shudders to think of what it would have meant to be put under quarantine. In this instance the c:ew and passengers beguiled the weary days by discussing some news the pilot had brought that the French and Spanish fleets had been destroyed at Trafalgar by Nelson. The passenger whose journal we have quoted was Lorenzo Dow, an American preaoher, who had been moved to cross the Atlantic to preach in England. The onl) woman on the vessel was his wife, Peggy. She, also, after the fashion of the times, kept a journal, and wrote in touching words her feelings at entering a strange land, where they had neither relative nor acquaintance. Lorenzo had hen drawn towards the little ship "Centurion," because she "belonged to a fair, steady Quaker," and he felt safe in engaging the passage thereon. But the confidence had not been reciprocated by the master of the vessel, and Lorenzo records that he hoisted sail and designed to leave without them, hut by hiring a boat for ten shillings and risking a dangerous sea the intrepid revivalist and his wife reached the" Cznturian, and were hoisted aboard. The misgivings of the seaman were not difficult to understand. Lorenzo Dow was indeed a striking figure. Pale and sallow in complexion, his face was deeply marked with smallpox contracted in Ireland a few years before. Contrary to the custom of the pariod he wore a long beard, and his hair had been allowed to grow until it reached his waist. A pair of eyes of unnatural brightness peered out between his locks, and held his hearers as by a spell. 'We need not wonder that he was misunderstood, for the strange exterior gave little clue to the EMI. He was eccentric to a fault, and his appearance declared it. It was in his preaching that the real man was revealed, and those who felt the glow and passion of his words never misunderstood and never doubted him. Though he had visited Ireland six years before, he had not been to England, and as he saw its green fields for the first time, he wrote in his journal:— We shall soon be at the pilot ground, and what will then ensue is now in the womb of futurity, but I expect to see the Providence of God in trials ; but how, when by whom or what means, I know not ; yet still I feel power to leave all to the Author of breath and Disposer of events. The expectation to "see the Providence of God in trials " was fully answered. Trials there were in abundance, but the lonely pair were not forsaken. Their small stock of money was soon spent at a lodging-house. They, were turned from this poor shelter heavy at heart, but a better door opened to them at the house of a printer in Edmund-street, whose wife felt a pity for them. Anxious to begin his work, Lorenzo preached his first sermon in a cottage in that street, and for several evenings held services and led the neighbours to a confession of Christ. This led to an invitation to preach at Zion Chapel, a building erected by the followers of Alexander Kilham. To this chapel Peter Phillips was drawn whilst visiting Liverpool to buy rushes for his chair-making trade, and so began an acquaintance which quickly ripened into friendship, and which held two remarkable men in a close bond until death divided them. We have three accounts of the meeting, and all of them record the fact that Peter was strangely impelled towards the chapel. In his own account dictated to his son, and etill preserved in manuscript, he tells us that he allowed the stage cpach to leave without him, and after the meeting stood on a form to ask if anyone would allow him to sit by their fireside through the night. Peggy Dow says in quaintly simple words that Peter" did not know there "was such a person as Lorenzo in the country, but feeling after he had done his business like he wanted to go to a meeting, and Wandering about for some tune he at last went into a meeting-house, where Lorenzo had been invited to preach." The circumstance meant much to Peggy, and she records with gratitude that it gave them a home, and made their stay in England memorable: "Peter Phillips and his wife were very friendly to us, and their hcuse was our home ever after when we were in Warrington." Lorenzo found himself in a Congenial atmosphere when he became acquainted with the ptople " called, in derision, Quaker Mothodists." They had then but one chapel at Friars' Green, with preaching stations at Stretton, Whitley and other villages, and these

quickly became centres of interest under the preach' ing of the American. ,After conducting revival services at Friars' Green; he yielded to the entreaties of those who had flocked to hear him from the villages, and began to make long tours in Lancashire and Cheshire. Those who know the districts will realise how severely he taxed his powers. In one day he speaks of preaching at Lymm, Appleton Thorn and Stratton. On another he begins with a sermon at Blackrod at twelve o'clock, preaches at Leigh, then at Lowton Common, and finishes the day with an indoor sermon at Friars' Green. As the travelling was done on foot, and the preaching for the most part done in , the open air, we can well imagine the strain upon , body and mind. When there was no walking he was equal to seven open-air sermons, each an hour in length, and in describing such a performance at , Warrington, says he felt much better for the effort. On the Lancashire side his journeys took him as far as Bolton, Chorley, Huyton and Preston. On the Cheshire side he did good work in many villages, preached in the salt pans of Northwich in the " Salt ' Country," and pushed forward to the "File Country." , Leeds, London, Bristol, Dublin, and the Midlands were also included in his large circuit. Returning one 'evening from a preaching tour in Cheshire and in company with Peter Phillips, he stopped by a sudden impulse and pointing to a barn expressed a desire to preach in it. The building was commodious and stood not far from the road side in the centre of the small village of Stockton Heath. By a coincidence we cannot hope to explain, the owner of the barn was considering the possibility of holding meetings in the barn, and without knowledge of what had 'passed in the mind of Lorenzo Dow, had sent him an invitation to preach there as often as he felt moved to do so. Both were deeply impressed by the circumstance, and the followthg Sunday found the barn swept and garnished by willing hands, its spacious centre filled with benches and borrowed chairs, and a company of fervent men and women to consecrate the building with their prayers. With the sermons of Lorenzo Dow the Quaker Methodist meeting at Stockton Heath began. The Church was fortunate in the fact that it was born in a great and true revival ; it was much more fortunate in the good men who were at hand to foster it. It would be quite true to say that it was the only Church to which Lorenzo Dow himself ever gave any real oversight. Like George Whitefield he reaped in any field, but never stayed to bind or gather. Only once in the long record he has left does he tell of devoting his time to the building up of a Church. The cir- cumstance which held him to the infant cause at Stockton Heath was the most painful incident of his life. Returning one evening from a tour in Cheshire he found his wife Peggy, dangerously ill of a fever. A child had.been born to them in Ireland, and whilst Peggy lay ill at Ship-yard the little one died at Stretton whither it had been removed. It fell to Lorenzo to wait upon his wife at night, and he tells us that for twenty-four days on end he did not take off his clothes. Unable to travel far afield he was able each evening to walk to Stockton Heath, and after preaching creep back to fulfil the heavy task of watching by the bed side of his wife. The revival work Of the first few months was valu- able and memorable, but there remained the no less important work of consolidation. This was done by the patient man who rocked the cradle of Independent Methodism—Peter' Phillips, and his less gifted but no less devoted brother, Joshua Phillips. Each Sunday through many years, one of the two brothers was present at the barn service. As soon as Lorenzo had sailed for America early in 1807, they decided upon the establishment of a Sunday School. With the large barn door as, black board Peter taught the children to repd and write. He taught them too, the mysteries of nausic, and instructed them in tins art of sacred songs. The barn school became famous and attracted children from afar. These came with a supply of food, and filled the intervals between the meetings by singing in the open air. That some pro- gress was nande in reading is shown by the fact that in 1808 a supply of Bibles was purchased. One of these is before us as we write and bears the inscrip- tion, " Stockton Heath Sunday School, May Bth, 1808." We have a special interest in this school for it was the first formed in our denomination. It was the first, too, that met in the county of Cheshire, and with one possible exception the oldest in Warrington and district. Many passed from the barn school to take up honoured places in the world, and the life stories of some of them if we could but record them, would intik° an interesting and inspiring record. The year 1828 witnessed the completion of "Pro- vidence Chapel." The name was conferred by reason of the happy chain of circumstances which made its erection po ' ssible and after twenty-two years of happy associations, and not without some regrets, the barn was relinquished as the place of meeting. The build- ing disappeared some years ago and we unfortunately have no picture of it remaining. It is quite impossible within the limits of a brief sketch to describe the succession of good men who have laboured in the Church and school at Stockton Heath. One name, however, cannot be left unmentioned. The famous Bridgewater Canal passes through the village, and shortly after its completion Thomas Eaton became the local manager. He married Ellen Balmer, of Hollin Edge Farm, Daresbury, and they made their home at a house which still stands on the old London Road. It would be iaopossibe to measure the influences which started at the home of these quiet and worthy people. Strongly interested in young men he opened his office as a night. school, and when this became too small the class was transferred to the barn at which he and his wife worshipped. At a later day the'place of meeting was the chapel, and in the task of teaching he was here ably assisted by his daughters. On week nights Thomas Eaton was the unfeed schoolmaster of the village, and on Sunday a devoted teacher and preacher in the barn Sunday School. Among the preachers he bore another reputation. His home was the " Pilgrim's Inn " for all who travelled in the service of the King. It was William Clowes who thus described it, and the title was never bettered. The foot travellers of a century ago knew the value of a wayside Pilgrims' Inn, and they never lacked a welcome here. As long as the journals of the early Methodist preachers are read the old homestead will not pass from memory. Among the preachers who stayed there the early Primitive Methodists were conspicuous. Hugh and James Bourne, William Clowes and James Crawfoot, all these have preached both in the barn and chapel. Of famous women preachers we may mention Dorothy Ripley and Agnes Patrick. The names of the temperance lecturers is legion, and we will only mention those whose names are everywhere remembered, George Harrison Birkett, Joseph Livesey, Fleury Anderton and James Teare. All these have been welcomed to the fireside of Thomas •

• Eaton, and have pleaded their cause in Providence`'. Chapel. To the broad sympathies of Thomas Eaton the Church owes the most famous chapter in its history —the formation of the first Total Abstinence Society within the walls of Providence Chapel. The story has been told in detail elsewhere. Disappointed in their attempts to find a building in which to hold a. temperance meeting, George Harrison Birkett and William Wood were urged to seek the interest of Thomas Heaton. These two Quakers deeply in earnest . in the cause of total abstinence, had issued a mani- festo to the people of Warrington and desired to follow it by the holding of a meeting. No bitilding was available for the furtherance of such a strange cause, and it was only by the favour of Thomas Eaton that the quiet chapel became available. We have hitherto taken it for granted that total abstinence at that date was a new teaching ; but the interesting fact has come to light that Soockton Heath was alread;to-. familiar with temperance meetings. A cottage which still stands was occupied by William Clarke, and his kitchen—large enough to serve as kitchen and work- shop —had already been the meeting-place of a temperance society. William Clarke was a filecutter by trade and was attached to the Wesleyan Metho- • dists. An able preacher he was offered at one period , the pastorate of Hill Cliffe Baptist Chapel. Between him and Thomas Eaton a close friendship existed, and they spent much time together and gave much thought to the welare of the village. Then as now a large brewing interest existed, and it was to counteract the influence of the brewery that they decided upon the holding of meetings for the instruction of the young . in the principles of temperance. We do not know the exact date of the formation of the society save that it was several years previous to 1830; nor have,any of . its documents been preserved. We know' however, that its Secretary was Mary Ann Eaton, a daughter of Thomas Eaton, and one of those who attended is still - spared to us in the person of Elizabeth Clarke. As we contemplate the strength and history of the , Temperance movement, it is difficult to realise that there should still remain in our midst one who signed the pledge at the first meeting of the first society. Elizabeth Clarke was born in 1818, and attended the meetings in her father's house. She was present at the famous meeting on April 4th, 1830, and at the close went to the table with her father and watched him sign the roll. As he laid down the pen she took , it up, and wrote her name beneath his. She was then a girl of twelve, and as a woman of eighty-seven remembers it vividly. William Clarke subsequently lived at a house opposite to St. Thomas's Church, which was known as Temperance House during his tenure. It is to him, and subsequently his daughter Elizabeth, that we owe the careful preservation of the Roll Books of the Abstinence Society. From December, 1830, the list is complete, and its existence has been sufficient to confirm the proud claim that the society formed at Stockton Heath was the first English Society of Total Abstinence. William Clarke lived to see his eighty-fourth year, and rests in St. Thomas's Churchyard.

, ' In 1601 the old chapel gave place to the new, and under the new and better conditions for meeting the 'Church began a new chapter in its history. The old I building remains, scarcely changed since it left the hands of the builder three-quarters of a century ago- Everyane will hope that it may be long preserved. and that its presence will be an inspiration to genera- tions yet to come. The task of maintaining villag° churches is always arduous, and at Stockton Heath the disappointments have been many. But there i 5 inspiration in a great past. TEACHING RELIGION IN SCHOOLS. Limiting CIE Tllll3 in Cheshire.

The Cheshire E lac:ition Committee, at a meeting at Crewe on NlorgLty, discussed the time that should be devoted to secular instruction in the elpmentary schools. The Director of Education reported that in some non- provided schools the amount of time set apart for religious instruction and observances was beyond the usual length devoted to such subjects, and he suggested that the committee should fix. the minimum time to be devotedto secular insti action during school hosrs. After a lengthy d senssion, it was resolved, on the, motion of Judge Bradbury, that the minimum length of time to be devoted to secular instruction should be— in infant schools, three hours, fifteen minutes per day where open four lions rier day, and where open beyond four hours the additional time so open ; andin schools for older children, 41 hours per day, or 232 hours per week.

NORTHERN RUGBY LEAGUE.

Bradford Warrington v. Salford—A Runaway Victory. The first engagement between these teams for the present season took place at Warrington on Saturday, before $,OOO pepple. Fine, dry weather prevailed, and the conditions generally were capital. Both sides suffered from absentees engaged in the County match. Warrington being without Hockenhall and Naylor, and Salford short of Lomas, Cookrane, and Warwick. 'Teams and officials. Warrington—Berry, back • Fish, Isherwood, Taylor, and Brooks, threequarter backs ; Jordan and Lees, half back ; Harmer;Preston, Boardman, Belton, Thomas, and Heath, forwards. Salford—W. Brown, back, ; McWhirter. Drake, R. Brown, and Hatnpson, threequarter backs ; Preston and John„rhalf backs; Rhapps, Spencer, flindshaw, Thomas, Nuttall and Lewis, forwards. I Referee, Mr. W. McCatcheon (Oldham). Touch judges, Messrs. W. Jones (Runcorn) and W. Bennett (Liverpool). , Preston started, and play opened directly in favour of Warrington. The game was only a minute old before Isherwood made the way clear for Thomas to score at the corner. Salford rallied, and their forwards quickly reached the Warrington line, only to find Fish in excel- lent form, a long run landing the game once more at the Salford end. Here Lees and Jordan opened out the game, and Fish taking a wide' Vass speedily outwitted the opposition, and scored beneath the posts, also add- ing the goal. Salford failed to make headway, and after Brooke had run well Preston scored, and added a goal. Near the interval, Fish placed a penalty goal. Half-time ;—Warrington, 3 goals and 3 tries (15 points), Salford nil.

Turning round, the visitors showed improved form, and John and Preston combining well Harnpson scored after clean passing. From this point the visitors fell away, and Warrington forcing the. pace Isherwood passed over to Fish, who rued in for a flue try. Preston added the goal. Lees next crossed after dribbling by Taylor, whilst in the last quarter of an hour Brooks scored two smart tries, and Preston added goals. Result :—Warrington G goals and 7 tries (33 points), Salford I try (3 points.)

ST. HELENS v. RUNCORN.

Played at St. Helens, before a good attendance. Play opened in exciting fashion, Atkinson saving on the line from a Runcorn dribble. St. Helens retaliated through Winters, garrison saving the situation with several players on top'of him. Runcorn then got a footing in the home half, and from a subsequent round of passing Deacon scored at the corner, Richardson failing to improve. Bocock, Creevey, and Winters then took play to the Runcorn line, where Winters dodged over. Creevey landed a goal. Resuming, Runcorn, with a splendid forward rush, took play to the home end, and by some tricky play Watton baulked Bocock and scored under the posts. Richardson failed at goal. Close on the interral the Runcorn backs indulged in a passing bout, from which Deacon scored under the posts. Moran bad little difficulty in converting. Half- time score :—Runcorn 1 goal 3 tries (11 points) , St. Helens 1 goal 1 try (5 points.) St. Helens started the second portion, and poor kick- ing by them caused their defence to be severely taxed. i Runcorn backs were repeatedly tackled close n, and eventually Flanagan got over near the posts, for Moran to place a goal. Runcorn again closed moo the Saints' defence. Deacon romping over at the corner, the goal hick failing. St. Helens roused themselves, and after a spirited run between Winters and Hillen the latter was tackled, and in "playing the ball" Briers picked up and dashed over the line, Creevey failing to impt eve. The Runcorn backs then passed well, and Flanagan slipped past Atkinson and scored, Richardson again failing to cnnvert. Result :—Runcorn :—Runcorn 2 goals 6 tries (22 points), St. Helens 1 goal 2 tries (8 points).

The home side coutinuel to have the best of matters,and Maddocks scored another try soon after the re-start, Baker again being successful with the goal kick. For a short time the home side were confined to their quarters, but eventually by short rushes they got ,to the visitors' half, wheT Garner picked up and dropped a good goal. After scrambling play on the home line Lunt scored for the visitors, no goal resulting Foster scored for Salford just before the interval. The Examiner, Saturday, October 13, 1906.

SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.

ABOUT IMARS.

Up to the present time human knowledge of Mars has been largely theoretical, the principal facts regarding the planet being that it is 141,500,000 miles from the sun, and approxi- mately 47,000,000 miles from the earth. The Martian year has 687 days. Mars is 4,230 miles in diameter, the earth being 7,918. Jupiter E 6,500, and the sun 866,400 miles.

===== 0002601_19061013_art0170.txt | included=true | included =====
A TEST FOR SEWER GAS.

The American Analyst gives the following test for the detection in an apartment of sewer gas: Saturate unglazed paper with a solution of one troy ounce of pure acetate of lead in eight fluid ounces of rain water; let it partially dry, then •expose in the room suspected of containing sewer gas. The presence of tho gas in any con- riderablo quantity soon blackens the test paper.

===== 0002601_19061013_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
SOLAR MARVELS.

zWe sometimes see a huge ring or halo round the moon, occupying a space in the heavens so largo that ninety moons' breadths would but just suffice to span it. Yet the body of the sun wpuld fill all that space ere we had approached within 2,000,000 miles of him. Once on his ap- parent surface, were we permitted to travel thereon, and with the speed of an express train, it would require five whole years of continuous journeying before we could make the circuit of his orb.

===== 0002601_19061013_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
A MONSTER ICICLE.

Niagara is not the only place where gigantic icicles are known. In the northern wilds of Canada a great cliff Or palisade, 500 ft. high, rises sheer from the deep waters of Lake Mas- sanoga. During a recent winter a cascade was transformed into a monster icicle hundreds of feet in length—a frozen fall, in fact, which stood out in striking and bold relief against the sombre darkness of the Laurentian" rock. There it hung suspended for several weeks, an object of wonderful beauty to the few hunters and lumbermen who saw it.

===== 0002601_19061013_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
SINGULAR SUN EFFECTS.

Curious bands of light and shadow have been noted momentarily as the sun rises and sets. For a special study of the phenomenon, Pro- fessor C. Rozet, a French observer, arranged a white screen to receive the rays on the rising of the sun from behind a hill, and found the bands very distinct, usually straight, but some- times undulating. They seem to be parallel to the part of the sun's edge that is just visible. They may be seen for two or three seconds: or more, after sunrise or before sunset, are about lin. or liin. wide, and travel sideways at greatly varying velocity—one to two yards and six to nine yards per second being the extremes. The velocity, says Science Siftings, seems to bear some relation to that of the wind.

===== 0002601_19061013_art0174.txt | included=true | included =====
NEW BEARING ALLOY.

. The high cost of tin has greatly concerned the makers of anti-friction meeals, who cast about For a cheaper substitute. Messrs. Siemens and Halske, of Berlin, according to the Vulcan, have patented a new alloy for this purpose. It is composed of 45-50 parts of cadmium, 45-50 parts of zinc, and up to 10 parts of antimony. The antimony added should net exceed 10 per cent., as otherwise the metal is too brittle. A very suitable proportion of antimony is 5 per cent. If the proportion of cadmium and zinc is considerably varied, the co-efficient of friction increases, and the other good properties of the alloy are essentially prejudiced. The mixture is claimed to be superior to the ordinary white metal.

===== 0002601_19061013_art0175.txt | included=true | included =====
ALZENE.

Two parts of aluminium and one part of 'zinc form an alloy to which has been given the name " alzene." It is equal in strength: says the Iron Age, to good cast iron, and superior to it in the matter of elastic limit. It takes a fine, smooth finish, and does not readily oxidise. The colour is white. It melts at a low red heat, and is very fluid, running freely to the extremities of the mould, and filling small or thin parts. Great care must be exercised in melting it. particu- larly when mixing the two metals, in order to preserve its smooth working qualities. It is somewhat brittle, and hence unsuited to such pieces as require the toughness possessed by brass. The tensile strength is approximately 22,0001 b. per square inch, and 3'3 is the specific gravity.

===== 0002601_19061013_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
LOST LUMINOUS EFFICIENCY.

The incandescent lamp fed by steam-generated current, is the greatest industrial extravagance of the age. Dr. Feuerbach cites the Cuban fire- fly's phosphorescence, which contains 99 per cent. of light and only 1 per cent, of heat, and contrasts this with the luminous efficiency of the incandescent lamp, which he finds to be only. 1 per cent. .iknpther electrician sh6vs that the in- candescent lamp consumes one-tenth horse-power for a light of sixteen candles, which is ten times the consumption of the electric strl for an equal effect. The energy of coal being 100 pex cent., it is eontinued, a first-class engine recovers 13 per cent., the electric generator of 95 per cent. efficiency makes available 12.35 per cent., and the incandescent lamp utilises 90 per cent. of this, or 11.11 per cent, of the 'whole. The lumi- nous rays of the lamp being 1 per cent., the light represents about one-tenth of 1 per cent of the energy stored in coal.

===== 0002601_19061013_art0177.txt | included=true | included =====
CORK PAVEMENTS.

A new cork pavement is favourably regarded by the municipal authorities in various cities, says Health. It is noiseless and soft to the tread, but at the same time durable. It is a variety of asphalt paving, in which cork is substitoted for the sand of the ordinary kind, preventing slip- periness, and deadening it to an even greater degree the vibrations' from passing vehicles. Snow does not freeze to it, and as it is non-ab- sorbent its qualities seem much superior to those of wood pavements. It is said to "stand" on heavy grades on which the ordinary asphalt pavement cannot be used at all, and still affords a perfectly safe footinA-. For schools and hospi- tals it would seem to be the ideal pavement, especially for courtyards, playgrounds, and streets around the buildine. For these purposes, the noiselessness and cleanliness of tho new pave- ment give it a marked advantage.

===== 0002601_19061013_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
WOOD PRESERVATION.

The ravages of the teredo on all descriptions of wooden piles, even if the wood is soaked in creosote, has been a great objection to the use of the wood for sea jetties, harbours; and dock work generally. The United States Railway Gazette has published a description of an inven- tion which it is claimed allows the use of wooden piles with complete immunity from the teredo. This, as described, consists of piping made of cement and divided longitudinally in halves, with keysvays in each section which, when placed around a pile and keyed, form a scan joint. This pipe is made in sections of 2ft. and 3ft. lone, sind the diameter varies according to the size of the pile. The pipe is placed around the pile at the top of the water, and section by section lowered until if rests on the bottom. The Pipe is then allowed to settle a few inches, and the annular space between the pipe and the pile is filled with sand. This casing. it is stated. af- fords absolute immunity from the teredo, whilst destroying. any that may already he infesting the iron moulds The Pining is made on the spot in moulds supplied by the patentees.

The result of his investigations is, that every gepPral illness diminishes the diameter of the hairs. The als h wsntincr r. +l,a

===== 0002601_19061013_art0180.txt | included=true | included =====
KEATINGS
