LETTERS TO. THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication mnst in all cases send their names and addtesses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith.

West Riding Judgment "Maintain and Keep Efficient."

Sir,—As Sir Robert Finlay has given it as his opinion I that the West Riding judgment is "erroneous," those who have been all along dubious as to its soundness may have less hesitation in venturing to express an opinion ' on what is, I think, the great defect in the pronounce- I ment. If a layman may presume to say so, the defect seems l to lie in the failure to draw any distinction between the " efficiency " which the education authority is respon- sible for as regards the schools, and that for which the teacher is responsible. Clearly these two responsibili- ties are altogether different and separate from each other. The local authority in a non-provided school is responsible for the interior maintenance of the build- ing, cleaning, Sm., for the equipment of the school, and for adequate staffing ; but it is not responsible for the actual teaching, which is in the hands of the teaching staff, who have to answer for that responsibility to the Government Inspector, who alone judges of the standardiil of efficiency attained. And if the school is certified to; be efficient by the Government Inspector, " kept up to I such a standard," to use the language of the judgment, "as would entitle it to claim the Parliamentary grant," the local authority are clearly altogether wrong in im- posing on any pretext what might be called a fine for inefficiency on the teacher. Somehow or other the judgment seems to say that if the secular teaching in a given school is certified to be efficient by the proper authority, that nevertheless that efficiency is cancelled by the addition of religious in- struction given by the teacher in accordance with the ant. Surely it is inconceiveable that the act shoul grant the permission to give religious instruction knowing it to be a drawback on the efficiency which the act was intended to secure; and there is no hint in the act itself that religious instruction was to be -regarded as such a drawback. Obviously the local authority in the West Riding has exceeded its province in attempting to set up a standard of teaching efficiency of its own apart from that of the Government; and it has also blundered in attempting to meddle indirectly with the religious teaching which is a thing specially reserved from their interference. It is still more serious blundering to lay down the position that religious teaching is detrimental to secular teach- ing in such a way as to cause it to be stigmatised as inefficient, though satisfying the inspector's standard of efficiency. And it is worse than blundering on the strength of these extraordinary positions to withhold payment from teachers for work certified by the proper authority to be done. Having regard to these considerations, having regard also to the pronouncement is the inferior court, and to the fact that the judges in the Court of Appeal were not unanimous, there seems to be a very stung case in favour of carrying the trial to the House of Lords.— Yours, itc., E. C. E. CARLETON. Holy Trinity Vicarage, Warrington, October 10, 1906. Mr. Morton and the I.L.P. Sir,—lt is disapnointing to find Mr. Morton making so many assertions devoid of any effort to back them up with the least possible proof. He has ignominiously failed to prove that the LL-P. has captured or attempted to capture our labour organisations. " It is a puzzle to the ordinary mind how a small minority can influ- ence the will power of a large majority of intelligent ' men connected with democratically conducted organisa- tions and make them "serve the ends of one political party" against their desire or wish and we are still left in the dark how the 1.L.P." feeds as parasite" on our labour organisations." It does not necessarily follow,to accept the principle of independent action, that you are compelled to he an I.L.P'er, or pin their programme on your hat. Of course the I.L.P.'ers have been the pioneers, the enthusiastic propagandists and teachers of self-reliance, doing their own work, when others promised but failed to perform ; unceasingly insisting on united political action, adopting the same method as our sagacious Irish friends and the idea is making such rapid and phenomenal strides amongst the masses that the " old school " that never had confldence,to walk alone but was graciously per- mitted to lean upon Liberal capitalist crutches for support naturally looks upon the trend of present events as a bitter pill to swallow, and in their chagrin and dismay the I L.P.is the largest target for their vitupera- tion. It will not be surprising if we are accused of attempting to capture the glorious illumination of the sun's rays for" one political party." Mr. Morton says "he has not, nor can he (Gasworker) controvert my assertion that these members (Liberal Labour) are there as free agents either to act independently or form a group to support or oppose any Government that may be in power." The question arises then why they have not acted independently when they possessed a free hand ; why have they attached themselves to the Liberal party ; why have they barefacedly supported Liberal capitalist candidates who have trodden on the workers when a living wage was demanded? And in arguing this matter we must remember the majority of Liberal- Labour representatives are paid their wage by Trade Unions. Yes.sir, audit is an indisputable factthat Con- servatism equally with Liberalism has a strong hold on trade union membership. Mr. Morton's contention that 'Labour's wants make them Liberals" will not stand impartial investigation. As a matter of hard dry fact from which there is no getting away there is more protective legislation standing to the credit account of the Conservative party on the Statute Book then there is to that of their opponents. It is a grotesque assump- tion that one side exists for the purpose of passing such legislation whilst the other does so grudgingly and nnder the force of compulsion. Plutocracy even when called Liberalism is no more the natural associate of democracy than Toryism. To claim that the Liberal-Labour mem- bers have exercised independence is preposterous. Before the last general election Mr. Charles Fenwick was announced in flaring placards to speak under the auspices of the ProgressiveLabour League but be was pre- vented from putting in an appearance for why? Is it not a fact he had to obey the crack of Mr. Herbert Gladstone's whip? The Liberals were in office in 1893. It was a time of exceptional distress through lack of employment. Surely the question of unemployed is a Labour question. In this session Mr. Kier Hardie intro- duced the following amendment to the address.—" But we desire humbly to express our regret that your Majesty has not seen fit to recommend the inclusion therein of such measures or measure as would have empowered the government and local administrative authorities to acquire land for cultivation and to set up undertakings whereby men and women unable to find employment in the ordinary labour market, might be profitably set to work." The Liberal-labour members on that occasion showed their independence by voting with their party against the proposal to even consider if something could not be done to appease the hunger of millions of women and children who were starving because the husbands and fathers were denied the opportunity to earn their daily bread. In 1903 Mr. Keir Hardie brought forward the same preposal ; 161. voted for the resolution and 201 against. The Liberal-Labour members and rich Liberals who had voted against it in 1893 all went into the lobby in support of it. What is the explanation. The simple fact that in 1493 Liberals were in office. In 1903 they were in opposition. We want a Labour party in the Commons that will vote for rights and will not hesitate to sacrifice a Government which shows a callous indifference to the sufferings of the people, a party that will use the legislative machinery in the interests of the workers, who are the nation, to bring about the longest. noblest,hapPiest and best life possible for all.— Yours &c., AS WORKER.

SOCIALISM AND PRIVATE PROPERTY. [By Mr. H. Whitham.]

Some very erroneous ideas are abroad as to what is meant by Socialism, The fact is, people who are not Socialists presume to say what Socialism is; they ven- ture to do this without even giving it that attentive thought one would think a movement that is gaining in numbers so rapidly should receive. I want also to say that even among Socialists there are too many who have but a hazy idea what it is they are support- ling, and speukhig in support of, and possibly I am one of the crowd. So far as my experience goes, I find the opponents of Socialism demanding a cut and dried programme.They want us to state explicitly,what it is we want, and how we are going to gain it. This I say is unfair, as no man can possibly say what the developments of to-morrow may be. I am willing to acknowledge that the Socialists have neither all the brains nor yet all the foresight, but of this they are certain : that unless there is a Ivery radical change made, and that very soon, our i so-called civilization will have proved itself a failure and life will certainly not be worth living. We lacknowledge the difficulty of our undertaking, but the ideal is grand. In the Royal Gallery of the House of Lords the British Premier recently made an epoch making speech in which he boldly proclaimed that there was no good in calling out "Peace, , peace" so long as the Governments of the world continued to spend such vast sums in the preparation for war. This is just where the Socialist stands. To gain fresh markets we declare war. These markets we want for two pur- poses; to make rich men richer, and to find employ- ment for the workers To be able to keep a leading position among the nations of the earth the very best among our brainy men must devote their lives to the construction of the murderous machines that slaughter on the battlefield and on • the sea. Stop all this criminal work, throw the brain, energy and money into the work the Socialist is doing, and then possibly you may have the right to call yourselves a Christian nation. Come over and help us. In my last I gave a -quotation from "Christian world " in which " J. 8," says,—True Socialism/proposes not the extinction of property but its universal diffusion. We would give every man his joy of individual possession. So far as my searching into the literature of Socialism goes this is a true definition of the word that is a " bugbear " to so many. The best leader of men is he who is gifted with foresight. The Socialist says we are simply "drifting." If we have a captain he is sleeping. He does not see that competition 'is dying and that rnon,opoly is taking its place, to be in its turn put onl one side by the grand "Co-operative Commonwealth." I Now we may ask, What's in a name? It is slid that under Socialism money will be abolished! This remains to be seen. There is no official declaration yet on this point. By money we can mean the com- modity, or the paper or the coin that passes in place of that commodity, which is in use as the standard of value sought in exchange for all other commodities. Money is therefore a very useful convenience for exchange, though imperfect now, and it seems probable it will be used for an indefinite time in the future, whether we call the circulating paper money or labour checks or what not. Socialism is not communism. " From each according to his ability, and to each according to his need is the law which rules, so far as capitalism permits, within the sacred bounds of home and family. When the social conscience is awakened the brotherhood of man will be a fact. Under present conditions we have but commercial cannibalism." Professor Ely has said that " Socialism is simply applied Christianity; the Golden Rule applied to every day life." Webster calls it—" A theory of society that advocates a more precise, orderly and harmonious arrangement of the social relations of mankind than that which has hitherto prevailed." In a brief article on ttie Co-operative Common- wealth, Mr. C. FL Kerr siys :—" When the Co-opera- tive Commonwealth is in operation, wages will tend to adjust themselves. If enough street cleaners cannot be obtained for fifty cents, an hotir, we shall have to pay sixty. If there are too many clerks at fifty cents., the pay may drop to forty until part of them have found work that is more in demand." This was issued with the formal approval of the National Committee of the U.S.A. A more direct and unmistakable declaration of ' the position taken by the Socialist party on this ques- tion could not be had. If there had been no develop', , meat of improved machinery, that is, if the people who use the tools could now own them individually there would be no demand for collective o,wnership of them. We can work with this machinery only collectively, not individually; therefore, if this social tool is to be owned by the people who use it, it can be owned by them only collectively. But this does not involve the ownership 14 the Government of all the means of production. It does not imply that there would be no occupations outside the govern- ment control and therefore no outlet for dis- content with government management. There need he and there should be no restrictions upon harmless private enterprises. In fact this common ownership of the things we depend upon in common, alone can make possible what the individualist calls a fair field of free competition. In it the private enterprise threatened by the great capitalists or prohibited altogether by the extortions of the trusts would again find its fair opportunity. And the skilful and indus- trious workman and the able and efficient bnsiness manager could each increase his income by increas- ing his productive power. It is very certain that a Socialist administration would not control all industry from one central point. The Socialist party always and everywhere leaves control in the hands of the smallest groups that can manage efficiently. I Again it would not take the artist's brushes, nor the farmer's little farm. We hold that tools sa complex , that they have to be used in common should be owned in common ; but if a man choose to use his own tools, there should be nothing in the world to prevent him, except the probable fact that as machinery improves 'it will be possible to earn more by working co-opera- tively thee by working alone. When I notice that our old friend " The Land Question" is once more trotted out I can but smile. We are far away beyond the land question. The struggle to free the land will shape itself into a struggle to free the toiler. We must make it impossible for a statement like the follow- ing to appear in the Press : 1 CHICAGO, September 3.—The directors of the Pullman Company expected to announce this week declaration of an extra dividend. The phrase " ex- I div." is a euphemism for a division of at least part of the organization's large surplus. The overphis doubtless has become not only unwieldly, but inconvenient from various standpoints, being a stimulus to the depredations attitude of certain statesmen, Legislative and Congressional. It is not well for a national corporation to have a bulging treasury these days. Last year the report of the sleeping car concern acknowledged a surplus of 22,151,940 dollars. That was a little over a year ago. Another statement is due for the period ended , July 31st. In the meantime the business of the com- pany is known to have bean unprecedented. In financial circles it is thought that the stockholders will get between 12,000,000 and 15,000,000 dollars in addition to their regular dividend. H. WHITHAII.

September 13th, 1900. _ 4) THE PIN MILL PURCHASE. Application for Alteration of Claim

An application was made by the Corporation of War- rington on Tuesday morning to the Deputy Vice. Chancellor (Mr. F. Willis Taylor) at Liverpool, for part of the statement of the claim in Colonel Edelsten's action with reference to the proposed purchase of Mersey Works to be struck out. Mr. Stuart Deacon (instructed by the Town Clerk of Warrington) appeared for the Corporation, Mr. J. Rutherford (instructed by Mr. Loingland) for the Nta,yor, and Mr. ft.' B. Lawrence (instructed by Mr. Herbert Hatton) for Colonel Edelsten. _

The primary claim of Colonel ErnestAn was to have the agreement of the 3rd December 1904, enforced against the Corporation or Mr. Bolton (the Mayor), but as an alterative he asked by his statement of claim for a declaration that the Corporation are bound either to purchase the whole of his premises or, at his option, to purchase so much of his land as lies between the pre- scribed line of frontage and Knutsford-road, and to pay him full compensation for damage, loss or injury sus- tained by him in respect of the whole of the premises, by reason of the Corporation having prescribed a building line through the premises. It was this alternative claim which the Corporation sought to have struck out on the ground that it embar- rassed them in their defence to the action. After hearing the arguments of counsel, the applica- tion was refused, and the Deputy Vice-Chancellor decided that he would not make the Corporation pay the costs of the application, but that they should abide the result of the action.

The Examiner, Saturday, October 13, 1906.

TREE PLANTING AT STOCKTON HEATH.

INDIGNATION OF THE PARISH COUNCIL.

Rural Council to be Approached.

After having remained in abeyance for several months the question of tree planting at Stockton Heath has amain come to the fore and the campaign promises to be renewed with great vigour. It was hoped, and confidently expected, that the visit last Friday, of the Executive Committee of the District Council Highways Committee would result in a settle- ment of this much vexed question being arrived at which would be satisfactory to all parties cOncerned. Such however has not proved the case, and the Parish Council is up in arms against the findings of the Committee which in effect mean that a large number of the existing trees have to be removed and that no more trees shall be planted in the future. A special meeting of the Council was held on Wednesday even- ing to consider the question when there were present:— Messrs. J. W. Bouchier (presiding), J. Whitley, T. R. Westbrook, H. J.Westbrook, J. Clarke, J. Hallows, F. Pearson and E.Bradshaw (clerk.) Mr. Bouchier explained that they and members of the Tree Planting Committee were invited to meet the deputation which visited the village and express their views. They, however, received what fie thought was very cavalier tleatment as in no single case were they asked to state their views and the findings of the com- mittee were given to them in the manner of the law of the Medes and the Persians. The Committee agreed to recommend to the District Council (1) flirt all trees on the north side of Wlaitefleld-road be removed and, with the consent of the occupiers, planted in the adjoining gardens, (2) that every alterative tree on the south side of the road be taken away and that stakes be substituted for the guards, (3) the position of the two trees in Dundonald avenue to be be altered, (4) all the guards lon the north side of Fairfield-road to be removed .and I stakes substituted. With regard to the south side of Fairfleld-road the committee decided that all guards must be taken up but ultimately decided that, for the present, they might remain. Then came the most serious part of the report which was that in the future no trees should be planted on footpaths of a less width than seven feet and subject to a sub-committee consisting of Messrs. G. Pilling, J. Whitley, and T. Baxter. The committee conceded that in Cawdor-street and Dundonald-avenue, subject to the approval of the sub-committee, the plant- ing of treesmight be continued. He pointed out to the committee that people resident in certsin streets where 1 the parapets were six feet wide might possibly be in 1 favour of trees being planted in the streets, and the committee replied that they could not allow any inter- ference with their highways. He thought that those who had made the streets and had to pay the rates to maintain them ought to have a voice in the matter, and say whether trees should be planted or not. Another difficulty was that the Parish Council had taken over the trees which were to be removed, but they had not the power to remove them, whilst the Tree Planting Committee were hot liable. Another point was that the whole of the tree-planting, which had entailed the cost of something like £.:80 had been done with the sanction of the District Council. As they would remember the District Council sanctioned the planting of trees under the supervision of the surveyor and the work had been done with Mr. Swinton's approval and therefore with the approval of the Dis- trict Council. Personally he felt very strongly on the matter and he thought the time had pretty nearly arrived when they were not to submit to the dictation of a lot of farmers who did not live anywhere near the village. (Hear, hear.)

Mi.Peaison thought that they had been very scurvily treated. They had been treated as though they were children and he must protest against it. He would also like to protest against the constitution of the sub- committee which had been appointed to decide the future planting of trees. Mr. Baxter had from the beginning been very fair. He had been an uncom- promising opponent of the scheme. Mr. Pilling had unfortunately sat on the fence. Mr. Whitley's bitter opposition to tree planting all along the line— Mr. Whitley,: Only on reasonable grounds. Mr. Pearson: To Mr. Whitley I firmly believe is due this most drastic resolution of the committee. I scarcely think it fair that Mr. Whitley should go into the room and adjudicate upon the matter knowing and acknowledging as he does, that he has been an opponent , of tree planting from its very inception. Mr. Whitley : Not on reasonably wide footpaths. Mr. Pearson : I repeat again that Mr. Whitley has been most unrelenting in his opposition. We are endeavouring to make the village as beautiful as we can and ought to receive support from Mr. Whitley whereas we have had nothing but the bitterest opposition from him. Continuing, he said he did not think it made the slightest difference whether a footpath was seven feet or six feet in width. If the principle was good for seven feet it was surely quite as good for six feet. (Hear, hear.) He thought the time was coming when they would have to endeavour to free themselves from the trammels of a somnolent District Council. The District Council was acting in a most reactionary manner and he thought they ought to protest most strongly. Mr. Whitley : I would like to know whether this meeting has been called for the purpose of discussing this question in a friendly, gentlemanly and business like manner or for the purpose of heaping abuse upon my head. If the latter I protest and shall retire. Mr. Pearson : I feel called upon to express what I think and I think I am justified in doing so. Mr. Whitley: I never moved or seconded any pro- position brought before the Executive Committee. Mr. El. J. Westbrook : I would like Mr. Whitley to give his opinion as to what was done in committee. Mr. Whitley : I have been independent and I am now. I have given you my views. I protest against my name being mentioned. Mr. Whitley continuing said he did not object to tries on footpaths seven feet wide. Mr. BOuchier : We all have strong feelings about the way in which the whole thing has been engineered. Mr. Bouchier proceeded to • refer to the planting of trees in Gaskell-street, Heath-street and Chapel lane. Those streets had been praised by the District Council. Mr. Whitley :1 never said a word atainst the planting in_Gaskell-street or Heath-street.

Mr. Clarke that byelaws provided for footpaths six feet wide. In face of that the resolu- tion of the committee with regard to a minimum widtl. of seven feet meant that in the future they would not be able to plant a single tree. Where Mr. Whitley came in was that knowing the bvelaws he must have known that the resolution would block all future planting. He hoped they would get Mr. Whitley's assistance and would like to know whether he would be agreeable to the alteration of the resolution to six feet. What struck him about the resolutions was their inequality. The trees on the north side of Fairfield-road were to remain whilst those on the north side of Whitefleld-road were to be removed. How on earth those two decisions could be reconciled he did not know because,he ventured to assert, Whitefield-road could give many inches to the Ship Canal side of Fairfield-road. The objection to the trees was that they were dangerous to pedestrians, but as the streets would now be lighted all night, anyone who ran into a tree would deserve to do so. The Dis- trict Council had approved of the trees, and why there should be that right-about-face, required a little more explanation than they had had. He wanted to be charitable, in fact, he nes prepa-ed to bankrupt himself with regard to charity, but for a man to say that he supported tree planting, and then in the same breath to say that they must not be put on footpaths less than seven feet wide was like saying that old age pensions should be given but none to a man less than 100 years eld. The whole thing was ridiculous as it meant that in future no trees could be planted, and to pretend to be favour of tree planting and to annex conditions of that sort was not sincere. Mr. Westbrook suggested that they should take one of three courses. They should call a ratepayers' meeting, send a resolution to the District Council or send a deputation to the District Council. Mr. Hallows suggested that a deputation was the wisest course. The question would be decided by the whole of the District Council and he was sure that when the Council understood the facts they would sup- port the Parish Council. He did not think the deputa- tion had power to appoint a sub-committee. At the same time time I shall use my judgment for the best interests of the parish. Mr. Westbrook : That is too general. lam prepared to make the village warm on the question and we shall see who is to be on these committees in the future. Mr. Hallows : Send a deputation and the Council will not be unreasonable. They are at present blinded by the dust that has been thrown in their eyes. At this stage the meeting became of an informal character whilst a resolution was being drafted. PROPERTY OWNERS' ASSOCIATION.

REPLY TO CRITICISMS. -- Opposing Municipal Extravagance.

The annual meeting of the Warrington and District Property Owners' Association was held at the Old Music Hall on Thursday evening under the presidency of Mr. W. Hickman. There was a fair attendance, includ- ing Mr. R. Lunt (hon. treasurer) and Mr. R. W. Cook (secretary).

===== 0002601_19061013_art0201.txt | included=true | included =====
THE REPORT.

The annual report was submitted as follows :—The subscriptions received for the past twelve months amount to £3l 65., and the expenditure £22 18s. Ud., leaving a balance of £8 7s. 10d., which has been added to £l3 18s. 4d. brought forward from last year. Total in hand and bank £22 6s. 21(1. Since the last report your Association has been affiliated with tfaa United Property Owners of Great Britain and Ireland, and deputations have attended Conferences at Manchester, Liverpool, and Barrow-in-Furness, at each of which they took part in the discussions of various measures before Parliament affecting the interests of property owners generally. Representatives of your committee attended the Local Government Board Inquiries at the Town Hall, respecting the old beam engine at Winwick, the loan for electrical purposes,and the proposed new street (between Sankey and Rylands-streets). The chief ()hints of the Association are to safe-guard the interests of property owners, and by carefupy watching public expenditure to encourage the development of com- mercial enterprize in the Borough. Elie committee appeal to all owners of property who have not already joined the Association to become members. The Associ- tion is non-political. It is pleasing to report an lincreased membership during the year 1906 of 73 against 32_of 1905. Total number of members, 240. . .

Mr. COOPER moved the adoption of the report and balance sheet. _ _ _

Mr. LUNT seconded and said they might compliment themselves very much on the balance sheet, especially when they considered that the expenditure this year had been greater than the income last. Nevertheless they were able to carry a fair balance forward. The motion was agreed to.

===== 0002601_19061013_art0202.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PRESIDENT ASSOCIATION'S POLICY DEFENDED.

On the proposition of Mr. COOPER seconded by Mr. THROSSELL, the meeting unanimously re-elected Mr. Hickman to the office of President.

I Mr. HICKMAN returned thanks and said he had not 'sought the position • in fact he had endeavoured to evade it. He would try, as he had done in the past, to further the interests of the Society during the I coming year. He had to congratulate the Society on the wonderful success they had achieved. They must not take it that because there was not a very large attendance at that meeting the Society was sickly, I weak, and about to expire. While they had been criti- cised, as all organizations were—they had had mud slung at them and severe criticisms passed upon them— he hoped that by this time it had dawned upon the "Dawn" that they did not exist in order to nip neces- sary reforms in the bud. They existed in order to do j useful work in the town and amongst the members of their own society. They were neither anarchists, revolutionists, nor obstructionists, but they were • pro- tectionists in the truest sense of the word, in order to protect their interests as property owners consistently with righteousness and justice. They had hada success- ful year numerically, and they hoped that the Society would make its power felt even to a greater extent in the future. The Society had been strengthened by joining the United Property Owners' Association of Britain, so that they had now some influence behind them. At the conferences which delegates from War- rington had attended matters of great importance to property owners -had been discussed. They included the taxation of land values and the powers it was sought to confer upon Corporations to enter into all kinds of trade aid commerce with the public money. Another subject which occupied considerable time was the loss which property owners suffered by night removals of tenants and the Conference was in favour of the intro- duction of legislation for the registration of furniture removers so that runaway tenants could be traced. It had been stated, continued Mr. Hickman, that the Association was the Non-Traders' Associa- tion. He wanted to emphatically deny that. They had Non-Traders as members, in fact they had all classes in the Association. It had been I alleged that the Association bad been formed for political purposes and that the Liberals, the Tories or the Non- Traders were going to capture it—in face all kinds of things were prophesied with regard to their organiza- tion which began in such a humble way and j had now 240 members, many of whom were the largest property owners in Warrington. The Associa- tion had not yet been captured by any political society or by the Non-Traders, Labour men or anyone else, and I as long as he was president it never would, for he would tight against that strenuously. There was room in the Association for men of all shades of opinion to unite to protect their interests, without injuring those of anyone else. In a few years time they hoped the association would be the strongest ratepayers' protection society that Warrington had ever seen. They asked for fair and equitable assessments all round, and for better police protection for their pro- perty in order that prosecutions could be taken to prevett the enormous amount of damage at present done. They also wanted to protect themselves against dirty, dishonest, and destructive tenants. The Associa- tion had been watching public expenditure on the property owners' behalf during the year. He wished to make it clear that they did not want to hamper the powers-that-be but when those powers proposed extravagant, unnecessary expenditure, they as property owners who had to bear the heavy share of the burden of taxation were going to keep their eye on that expenditure and raise their voices in protest. Mr. Hickman referred to the opposi- tion of the Association to the compounding of the beam engine at Winwick, and the construction of the 40ft. street between Rylands-street and Sankey-street, and said he considered their opposition had weight in those matters. A similar remark applied to the proposal to erect a new block of buildings at the %Workhouse, which they held to be absolutely unnecessary. It was worthy of note that the suggestion which he (Mr. Hickman) made for the utilization of the old infirmary had been carried out at a cost of less than £3oo,whereas they were originally asked to pay £8,500 for the shell of a new building. The Association were willing to help the powers-that-be by supplying any information which might be of service. (Applause).

===== 0002601_19061013_art0203.txt | included=true | included =====
OTHER ELECTIONS.

Mr. IL Lunt was thanked for his past services and re-elected treasurer and Messrs. John H. Gittens and Elias Griffiths were re-appointed auditors. The follow- ing were elected on the committee :—Councillors Burton, Pemberton, Skelland, Evans, Lord and Wilding, Messrs. Cooper, Throssell, Crompton, Diggle, R. Isher- wood„R. Lee, J. T. Barton, d. Thornton, FLWinstanley, Marsh, Hill, Hewitt, W. Winstanley and J. T. Whittle. Votes of thanks were accorded to the Chairman and committee and in responding for the committee Mr. COOPER said that many matters in Warrington required watching, and particularly what had been going on i the Council during the past fortnight. Things had been done which he (Mr. Cooper) considered ought not to have been allowed.

Councillor PEMBERTON said there was a great work for property owners to do, without in any way going against the interests of the individual ratepayers. The property owners' interests were so identical with those of the tenant that it was almost impossible for a good landlord to separate them. The criticisms were proof that the Association had done good work. It was decided that after the annual Conference of the United Property Owners' Association of Great Britain a meeting should be held to receive a report from the Warrington delegates.

The only monument to Captain Cook in New Zealand was unveiled on Monday, in the presence of a large gathering of both races, at Poverty Bay, the spot where the discoverer first landed.

The strikers at the lumber mills at Buckingham, near Ottawa, on Monday, attempted to interfere with the working of the mills. The police fired on the demon- strators, killing three and wounding eleven.

Salford are, naturally, thirsting for revenge and will not have long to wait for the opportunity, for the return match is due to take place at the Willows in a fortnight. They will need to make a big improvement all round if they are to reverse the result of Saturday's game, for Warrington were immeasurably superior. Last season, too, Warrington won at Salford notwith- standing a couple of tries from Lomas, and they mean to repeat the performance. It is interesting to note that in the last three weeks &Ilford have had about 90 points scored against them Either the new rules do not suit the team, or there is new blood required in considerable quantities.

The most marked superiority of Warrington on Saturday was at three-cluarter, and there is no doubt to-day the club possesses the best quartette it has had for many years. Even when Dickenson and Harris were at their best they never quite balanced Isher- wood and Fish, whereas Taylor and Brooks make an equally dangeious pair as the more famous left wing. They have a 'better understanding than their predecessors and this results in tries. To-day the defending side against Warrington has something to do in order to keep both Fish and Brooks in check. With the forwards doing their work properly it is impossible to prevent one or the other scoring occasionally, and this penetrating power should lead Warrington to giddy heights before the end of the season. The defence has always been pretty sound, and was never better than now. Warrington's score of 126 points to 41 is one of the best in the League, and the fact has to be remembered that there are no " rabbits " among the victims yet.

It is difficult to say much about the Salford match, for all the home players distinguished themselves. True, the forwards did not heel out as well as might have been in the first half, but they nride amends later on, especially when shorthanded. In the loose they quite outclassed the Reds, and some of their !following up, passing and running, was reminiscent of the New Zealanders. By the way I am becoming more and more convinced that the team which makes the best copy of the "All Blacks' " methods will be the most successful in this season's competition. 11 don't refer to the five-eights formation—though that has its good points especially at 13 a side—but to the passing and backing-up. Kicking is practically use- less, except in an emergency, and our all-conquering visitors almost dispensed with it in their memorable campaign. The other side might kick like a mule. but the scientific "All Blacks ' merely fielded the ball and started a passing run which gained twice as much ground as a return punt would have done. Warrington did less kicking and more passing en Saturday with excellent results. I hope they will cultivate the combined play more and more. They are just the side to make a success of it. I

Comment on individuals is almost unnecessary, but it is worth while praising Berry's soundness, Brooks' pluck, and Fish's return to form. Jordan, too, gave a creditable display at half, while Thomas was like a miniature Gwyn Nicholls at times. He must not forget that he is a forward though. On the side of the sad Salfordians there was not much to enthuse about. The forwards pushed well in the packs, and the halves, Preston and John, were very nippy, but the three- quarters were woeful. The wings were better than the centres, and Lomas was • sadly missed. Billy Brown did well at full-back and showed a bold front every time to Warrington's dashing raids.

For the Lancashire Cup-tie with Leigh to-day War- rington are expecting to be absolutely at full strength. They have something to Wipe off the slate, for in the corresponding engagement at Warrington last year Leigh snatched a . victory. Then, Warrington were handicapped by injuries to players, etc., whereas now the boot is on the other leg and on present form the homesters should secure a 20 points victory at least. In my opinion,Warrington and the present holders will have a considerable voice in the destination of the Cup. Wigan may have a hard task at Salford, but they sliDuld win.

There is only one change in the Warrington team from last Saturday, Hockenhall taking the place of Jordan. Lancashire did an excellent thing by winning the match with Cumberland at Whitehaven on Saturday. The team looked rather weak on paper, and most people expected that the Cumbrians wonld secure a

victory. In fact the Saturday evening papers gave the final score : Cumberland 15 points Lancashire 4 points, and many people did not know till the follow- ing day that these figures should have been reversed. The new men on the Lancashire team gave a capital account of themselves, and the combination proved too good for the home lot. LTockenhall did very well on the whole, though Jolley was the star half-back. Leytham got a couple of tries in brilliant style, while Naylor was always in front of the forwards and played a rare bustling game.

The "A" team sustained their first defeat of the season at Salford on Saturday and as it was in a 'Combination match the disappointment was keen. Salford " A " won by 23 points to 9 points, but as only a shadow of the regular Warrington 13 was able to play, the result was not quite unexpected.

There is atother point which contributed largely to the defeat. Harris and Dick nson were selected to play but both " struck "for more money and substitutes had to be found at the last moment. It was unsports- manlike of these two players t.i throw the club into such a difficulty at a critical juncture, and I am glad to learn that severe punishment has been meted out. At the Committee meeting oa Wednesday, Harris was suspended for two months and placed on the transfer list at the end of that time, while Dickenson was sus- pended for a fortdight. I think the Warrington Club has alyrays dealt fairly with its players, and it has a right t¢ expect honest service in return. The men are professionals now, and any flouting of authority could not be tolerated.

• -- The "A " team h e no match to-day; but on Saturday next they en rtain Salford " A" and should square accounts with them for the season. Lambert got hurt again last Saturday, but is hoping to be right for the return game.

The Leigh team to play at Warrington to-day has been chosen as follows :—Clarkson, full back ; Neville, Bennett, Roscoe and S. Johnson,threequarters; Burgess and Whittaker, half-backs; Lee, Winstanley; Blackburn, Webster, Smith and O'Neill, forwards.

As will be seen from, our advertising columns the Warrington Club announces the promotion of a Schools Rugby Football Competition. The knock-out principle will be followed, and handsome prizes given to winners and runners-up. Schools within a four [ miles radius of Warrington may enter their names for the Competition with Air. H. Ashton on or before the 31st inst. The Club will provide all playing ()Mitts with the exception of boots. This is a wise move on the part of the Club, but it is a pity it was not made before the Schools Association League was formed. However, I hope and think it will "go " with proper encouragement.

THE NEW COUNTY COURT JUDGE.

Welcome at Warrington.

A hearty welcome was extended to His 'Honour Judge Shiress Will, K.C., the newly appointed County Court Judge of No. 7 Circuit, on his first appearance at the Warrington County Court on Thursday. The legal representatives in attendance were Messrs. F. Cuthbert Smith and Goidle (barristers, Liverpool), H. J. Longton, A. Browne, E. A. White, W. J. Forshaw, F. G. Taylor, J. W. Knowles, C. H. Unsworth, T. S. Steel, A. J. Willett, S. Wallhead and A. T. Hallaway (deputy town clerk.) Mr. Cuthbert Smith, on behalf of the Bar, extended a very respectful and hearty welcome to his Honour. He (Mr. Cuthbert Smith) had the privilege of saying some words of welcome at Birkenhead on Tues- day, but whereas at Birkenhead there was practi- ally one Bar, at Warrington two Bars met. The Liverpool Bar and the Manchester Bar both practised in the Court and he (Mr. Smith) on behalf of the barristers of each city assured His Honour that they would in every way assist the Court so far as in them, lay in the discharge of their duties. It was impossible on such an occasion to have apart from them the memory of one who occupied the Bench so recently. To every one of theft the late Judge Bowen Rowlands was not only a great judge, but a kindly friend, and the youngest member of tha profes- sion felt that in him he had one who would give him every assistance, and the humblest suitor in the County Court, although unrepresented, felt that at the hands of His Honour he would receive a kindly hearing. They grieved—how deeply it was difficult to say—at the loss of a kind friend, but with that grief was mingled the happiness of welcoming the new Judge. Mr. H. J. LONGTON on behalf of the solicitors' branch of the profession practising in the Court, said he had great pleasure in extending to his Honour their sincere and hearty congratulations upon his appointment. They hoped therelations which had already existed so happily between members Of the profession and the occupants of the Bench would long continue. They would endeavour to give all the assistance that lay in their power to facilate the satisfactoryand expeditious trans- action of business. They could not allow that oppor• tnnity to pass—as they had no Law Society in the town through which they might more officially express their feelings—without alluding to the loss they had sustained by the death of Judge Bowen Rowlands. They felt at all times when they appeared before him that they had in him a gentleman who would listen to them courteously and would give to every part of the case careful attention. His personal charms were very great and he was exceedingly kind to them on every occasion. Mr.T.J.Rinc WAY (Registrar)on his own behalf and on behalf of the officials associated himself most cordially with the remarks of Mr.Cuthbert Smith and Mr. Longton in welcoming his Honour and expressing their deep regret at the death of Judge Bowen Rowlands. His Honour thanked the speakers most heartily for I their kind welcome. He sincerely hoped that the good 1 relations which had existed between the members of the Bar in the past would continue. It would be his pleasure to foster that good feeling and he thanked them heartily for their good wishes. Everywhere he had gone he had heard bind and affectionate words in remembrance of there late Judge. He did not wonder that they should hold him in such kindly and honoured remembrance because he (His Honour) knew him well, and be believed a more fair-minded man never lived. He was sure their kind words would be most gratifying to those whom their late Judge had left behind.

' THE SOAP COMBINE.

250,0)0 People Affected.

It was stated on Wednesday (says the "Duly Des- patch '') that the following firms have definitely entered into an understanding with a view to complete a com- bination in the soap trade Messrs. Gossage, Widnes. Crosfield and Son, Warrington. Lever Bros., Port. Sunlight. Ogston and Tennant, Aberdeen and Glasgow. Christopher Thomas and Bros., Bistol. Watson and Sons, Leeds. For all practical purposes, it is believed, Messrs. Watson and Sons and Messrs. Lever Bros. are already amalgamated, and one of the principles of the Port Sunlight organisation is in London, it having been agreed by Lever Bros. to look after the whole of the London work for Messrs. Watson as well.

About 250,000 .w.o—ripo—ple,iii MESSRS. CROSFIELD'S POSITION.

We understand that a further Conference with refer- ence to the scheme took place at Leeds on Thursday and that Messrs. Croslield were among the firms repre- sented. The directors of Messrs, J. Crosfleld and Sons, Ltd., who are actively concerned in the project, have with others who are acting with them, pledged them- selves to secrecy until such time as the arrangements have been completed. We are however, informed that what is in contemplation is a workinm arrangement between certain firms, with a view to diminishing the increased price of production. This increased cost, of raw materials it is stated has brought about a really critical situation which will have to be met as much in the interests of the public as the soap manufacturer. The name of Messrs. William Gossage and Sons, Limited, Widnes, ha, been during the last two days persistently mentioned in London journals as one of the companies which have joined the ring of soap manufacturing firms. A Press representative on inctiir- lug at the Widnes works, was informed that no authority had been given for the inclusion of Gossages in the published list of combining or probably combining firms. Neither was the Widnes company represented at the private conference of soap manufacturers which was taking place at Leeds.

ELEVEN FIRMS ARRIVE AT AN AGREEMENT The "Evening Chronicle " says : The soap trade combine became on Thursdayan accomplished fact. As a result of a private meeting of manufacturers at Leeds, what is said to be an authoritative announcement is made. The scheme is said to be nothing, but an arrange- ment to secure efficiency and economy of administra- tion. What is hoped from the combination is a large saving in the expenses and salaries of travellers, adver- tisements, and the carriage of goods. One source of ecdrieruy under the scheme will be that, instead of each firm having its own travellers in a particular district, one traveller in each district will represent the- whole. The question of giving prizes for wrappers has inci- dentally come under consideration, and it is felt that this practice will have to be abolished. The system, if popular in many poor households, has been obnoxious to some of the retailers, and has—so it is represented encouraged dishonesty OR the part of servants.

TAXATION OF LAND VALUES. Meetings at Warrington.

Meetings at which the taxation of land values was, advocated were held at Warrington on Thursday and last evening, and at both addresses were delivered by Mr. Frederick Verinder, secretary of tile English League for the Taxation of Land Values. Thursday's meeting which was held at the Liberal Club, was unfortunately only poorly attended, but those present highly enjoyed the proceedings. It would seem unlikely that such sa apparently dry theme could be made really entertais; ing hut Mr.Serinder treated his subject in a masterfsl manner and extracted from it the maxitnum of humoig _

with which to drive home hard facts. Throughout kept his audience constantly highly amused by his qua illustrations, -while at the same time he gave plenty food for *enaction.

Mr. WORRALL presided and remarked that ,Ilhey-on doubt the present condition of the land question we, very unsatisfactory and they all realized that somethisg must be done to remedy that which,in the eyes of man) was nothing but a glaring piece of injustice. (Heat hear.) Mr. Si. R. LESTER M.A., said that in the past we ha( allowed a few individuals to monopolise the gifts 0 nature in the form or land and we were paying tbi penalty of having made that mistake an 4 were drivel to take from industry to support the land owners. Lan' was provided by Providence for the use of mankind an( every man born into the world had a claim ti

a right to use it. The question itrOie how was that -right to be secured? It was o b vious that the holders of the land possessed an advantage over everyone else and ought bf meaes of a tax to be forced to compensate those who had not got the land to the full value of his holding. That was known as the single tax and its adoption would mean an end to poverty, distress, and undeserved unemployment as by abolishing the private ownership of land the tax on industry would be removed. (Applause). Mr. VERIND ER said the question would probabtr arise, What were land values ? Suppose, he said, thai` in Warrington all municipal work was stopped and tbe town became such a pestilential place that all its inhabitants were forced to leave it. After their depay;

tuse, it was obvious that the land of the town wOul cease to have any value. (Hear, hear.) Suppose 0 the other hand that the. town was made a m.. 41! . . _

---- - ----. --•,....... .r....,. ...- „,„ - - .• -- ----- - desirable place to live in and that they held a 10s. house. Men from Manchester would seek residence here and would offer 15s. for the house with the result that thel would have to pay 15s. or leave. it was like a con' juring trick. Warrington opened its arms to receive an advantage but somehow or other when they came to count their advantage they found it in the pockete of the landlords. Of all questions land was by far the most important. Land and labour wdre the two great things in production. Capital was useful but it was not essential. When he spoke of capital, he of course, referred to machinery and they could take the biggcst machine they could find and no part would they dis- cover wnich was not the product of land and labour. It therefore followed that if all the present capital rrBs lost in a comparatively short space of time man, with , free access to the land, would create fresh capital. If they studied any social or economic problem and got I to the bottom of it they would find that it rested on II land. Mr. Verinder proceeded to speak of the creation of I land values. To illustrate his point he pictured a man thrown into a trance in the stone age and waken' jog up in 1906 to find himself the owner of an acre of , land in the heart of London. The land which he held I would be enormously valuable but what had the mull / done to create the value? He regretted to have to sal ' it but the man had done just as much as any other landlord—nothing at all. All he had to do ' I was to wait whilst the valuewas created by competitiou for the land, and then to pocket the value. He would '4 not trust his own brother or even the arch angel , Gabriel with the power which they gave te the men whom they allowed to monopolise the land. Other monopolies were as nothing. Talc° , the railway monopoly. It would be inconvenient but not fatal to their lives to do without the railway!.

The same thing could be said of every other monopol., except land and as to attempt to do without the lane would be fatal in that instance they wore absolutely at the power of the monopolist. They were agitating their minds over the question of unemploS- ment. They would never solve that question by coto' mittees and labour bureaus but must get to the bottom of it and when they arrived at the seat of the trouble they got down to the land. Unemployment was a Ties- tion of land and labour and so long as our land laws prevented a fusion of the two so long would we hove

unemployment. The thing that had to be alt was the land system. (Applause.) Votes of thanks to the speaker and to the Chair concluded to the meeting.

MEETINGS FOR MEN.

, ---- In spite of the heavy downpour of rain, Tote Assembly Room was full on Thursday evening, at the first of the services for men, conducted by the Rev. J' F. Matthews. Mr. Alfred Grounds presided at the piano, and Mr. Levi Richardson sang very effectivell " Blind Samson." After briefly explaining the origi° and purpose of the gathering, and of those which were to follow, Mr. Matthews gave an address on " What IS religion 9" It was not his object, said the speaker, tc,'l give a definition of religion. Writers on philosophysn' religion gave many definitions. He called attention to various phenomena of life, and showed how on 0,13 one hand,there were clear evidences of the human spirit desire and approach of, the Eternal. The act °I worship In any form assumed God, and God was capable of being known, and man was capable of knowing o°l' On the other hand religion so understood, involved alS° tho recognition of earthly relationships and responsibili- ties. Both carried obligations. Religion was, there" , fore, essentially ethical, but ethics did not constitute the whole of religion. Some men , had no ethical sense at all. They wore degenerats of the worst type. Instead of carrying out theit functions as sons, fathers, husbands and citizens; they 'had to he taken in charge by the State, an° deprived of liberty until certain sure signs of reforrun' ) tion were forthcoming. Religion had to create a nen' J ethical sense. It had, in fact, to re-create the may; e Religion was ethics, plus a God-inspired, God-directea ' emotion, leading man to fulfil the highest obligatioa9 in every capacity of life. To them, however, religiotl was always and everywhere associated with the name° Jesus of Nazareth. In Him were united, as in no othe the supreme passion for righteousness, with a complet i apprehension of the Divine Will and purpose. Ile I was always glad when he heard men say they made 13°13 I profession of religion. That was,they attended no churn, or chapel, recognized no stereotyped form of religion,: meeting. The less profession the better. He hopen : however, the saying was not offered as an excuse for th° neglect of the cultivation of personal goodness and Of , I practice of all those unselfish arts and habits rendered life so beautiful. Worship had its value 1...,-. helping men to be and to do. By all means let then; he practical ; let personal goodness assert itself, P% sonal service, unselfish and pare, move them to all tu interests of their fellows.

PROGRESSIVE LABOUR LEAGUE. OPENING OF THE WINTER SESSION. Tte winter session in connection with the Warring- ton Progressive Labour League was opened on Tuesday evening when a well attended meeting was held at the Liberal Club, under the presidency of Mr. T. Samosa. The first business was to elect a treasurer to fill the vacancy caused by the retirement of Mr. E. Osborne, and to elect a member to a vacant seat en the Executive Committee. Mr. S. Wright 3.P., was unanimously elected to the first office whilst Mr. E. W. Jones, by a large majority was appointed to the Executive Committee.

The death occurred suddenly on Thursday morning , of Sarah Miller, aged sixty-six, wife of John Miller,ll, Dial-court, Warrington. Deceased followed the occu- pation of a " knocker-up," and, after completing her I task, she returned home about 7.15. Later, whilst sitting on a chair, she was seized with a violent fit of scoughing, and, breaking a blood-vessel, died I almost immediately. Illegal Removal of Swine. Walter Payne, a farmer, of Wrinivick-, near Warring- , ton, was at Newton-le-Willows on Thursday fined ss. ' and oosts for removing swine illegally. It was pointed out that the swine had been removed from Chester to defendant's premises, and that he had removed three within the prescribed period of twenty- eight days. Superintendent Cocker did not press the case, as such instances were very rare in Lancashire. HARRY LAUDER'S LIFE STORY.—The King of Comedians is tellitv, in his own amusing and inimitable style the telling of his life in the "Liver- pool Weekly Post," the popular paper, commencing on October 20th. Don't fail to buy it and judge for yourselves.—Advt. Shocking Fatality at Lymm. A shocking fatality occurred at Lymm on Thursday afternoon. Charles Hoult (36)a single man, of Booth's Hill, Lyinin, was attending a threshing machine which was proceeding to the farm of Mr. Massey. In t ,e yard a cesspool had to be crossed/and the weight af the engine must have injured the dome as when the presser was passing over it gave way. The presser capsized as a result and in falling pinned Hoult to the ground. When extricated it was found that Hoult was dead, his injuries being of such a torrible nature that he must have succurnbad almost instantaneously. Lecal Baker's Success. At the Bakers' and Confectioners' Exhibition that has just been held in Tendon, Mr. :C. E. Toft was again awarded, for the third time, the 50 Guinea Chalrenge Trophy for a collection of bread in a com- petition open to Bakers and Confectioners in the United Kiagdorn. "Compton Dene " in his criticism of the Prize Bread at the Ethibition, says in the " Baker and Confectioner " of September 23 :—" The colour was grand. It cut well and the outside appear- ancr was everything that could be desired. It was tastefutly got up and nicely baked, proving a very interesting and instructive exhibit, and Mr. Toft is deserving of great praise for having again secured the cup which he held in 1900 and 1902.

Rylands Bowling Club's Success. The Bowling Committee of Messrs. Rylands Bros.' Workmen's Recreation Club held a tea and smoking concert at the Cemetery Hotel on Saturday evening to celebrate their winning the Finlayson Cup and gold medals offered in connection with the Warrington Works Bowling Association. A most enjoyable even- ing was spent thanks to the efforts of Mr. I. Newton (hon. secretary) and Mr. Humphreys. A large com- pany partook of a capital repast provided by Mr. and Mrs. Allison, and the subsequent pro- ceedings were under the presidency of Mr. W. Peter Rylands, with Ur. G. Holbrook, the captain of the bowling, team, in the vice-chair. During the evening congratulatory speeches were made the team being highly complimented on their victory, whilst loyal and other toasts were honoured. An entertaining musical programme was submitted songs &c., being given by Mr. T. Humphreys Mr. W. Moores, Mr. T. Reddish, Mr. W. Whittle, Mr. Holbrook and Mr. J. Warburton. Mr. George Anderton was an efficient accompanist. -A vote of thanks was accorded the host and hostess, on the proposition of Mr. G. Holbrook, seconded by Mr. Humphreys. Sale at Cuerden Hall. Mr. R A. Naylor having decided to reside on his Somersetshire estate, i great dispersion sale will take place at Caerden Hall, .;;Thelwall, on the seventeen business days commencing Monday next. The sale is arousing a great deal of interest for it is rarely that such a large collection of the choicest objects of interest to the connoisseur is offered for sale by auction,. Mr. Naylor has been a liie-long collector of works 44 art ; pictures, china, and furniture having received his close and particular attention. The paintings, engravings, water colours, and coloured prints which adorn the very large hall and the walls of most of the looms of the mansion 'are exceedingly numerous and include many works by the old masters. The china is especially rich in old services, vases, and figures, and contains specimens of almost all the coveted designs. The furniture, some of which has been seen at South Kensington, contains many speci- mens of the genuine antique ; while the curios extend over a wider field than usual. The public view which began on Thursday will be concluded to-day (Satur-, day), admission being by catalogue to be obtained from the auctioneers, Messrs. Capes, Dunn, and Pilcher, 8, Clarence-street, Manchester. Warrington Apollo Choir's Success. The most notable triumph to be yet recorded by the Warrington Apollo Prize Male Choir was achieved on Saturday, when they were placed second to the Man- chester Orphens Glee Society in the choral competition at the Blackpool Musical Festival. Speaking of the test pieces the " Manchester Guardian " critic says: " They weie of quite unusual interest; first came Peter Cornelins's " Ach iris nichtig, ach wiefluchtig" (op. 9, No. I),a solemn five-part song based on a melody by Melchior Franck, who flourished at Coburg in the sixteenth century and was an exceedingly prolific com- poser of church music. The colouring of the three bass parts, although somewhat sombre, is wonder- fully rich and effective. Probably the cultivation of the part-songs of Brahms and Cornelius during recent years has done more to draw out the latent interpreta- tive powers of choirs and conductors than any other work that has been prescribed hy the various festival authorities; each work selected from these composers seems to draw forth qualities hitherto little suspected. M'Dowell's " Dance of Gnomes " was in violent con- trast—short; fast, vivid, decisive, grostesque. The evening pieces were Strauss's ' setting of "Der Branttanz ' from Herder's " Stinamen der Volker." Technically this was perhaps the most difficult thing to sing, owing to the elusive character of some of the harmony. Lae Williams's "To Celia" was in a lighter and more piquant vein and was rendered by the better choirs with full appreciation to its sly humour. Workman's Compensation Case. At the Warrington County Court on Thursday before his Honour Judge • Shiress Will, KC., Mr. Cuthbert Smith, barrister (instructed by Mr. T. S. Steel) made an application under the provisions of the Workmen's Compensation Act, 1897, on behalf of Frederick John James, smith and mechanic, 9, Lontzshaw-street,for his Honour to "register" an agree- ment arrived at with his employers, Whyman's Foundry C)., Ltd., with respect to compensation for injuries received. Mr. Browne represented the res- pondents. Mr. Cuthbert Smith said the respondents sent to the Registrar a notice that they disputed the con- tinuance of the payments and the Registrar accordingly gave,notice to he applicant on the 16th August. The -matter had, therefore, to be decided by his Honour. The agreement come to between the applicant and the firm was to the effect that compensation at the rate of 14s. Sd. a week as from the 22nd May should be paid. The only question for, the court was whether there ever was such an agreement—Mr. Browne : We say there was an agreement to pay 14s. Bd. a week for the tem- porary disablement of the man,but he has since been fit to work, and therefore there is no agreement now. There is no writing at all—Mr. Cuthbert Smith: It is a veibal agreement, Mr. Smith explained that the injury was caused by a piece if steel flying into one of the applicant's eyes.—Applicant gave evidence and denied that there was an understanding that he should only receive half the amount of his wages during the time that he was totally incapacitated. He expected to receive the money if his sight was not restored. Mr. ' Browne: Was there the slightest Understanding between you and Mr. Whyman that there should be a permanent payment? Applicant : There was DO understanding. I expected to receive the money till I was fit to work.—Mr.Browne contended that applicant ought to bring a claim for compensation in the usual way.—The Judge said that if there was no hard- ship attending the course urged for the applicant he would register the agreement, but if he did so it appeared from the Act that the applicant could go on claiming and issuing execution until the agreement had been altered. The applicant would not be in any way prejudiced if the agreement were nat ?tcorded as he could bring a claim for compensation which would be decided on its merits. Half weekly wages would be allowed for the time he /was incapacitated and the amount he was subsequently entitled to would be duly settled. He declined to register the agreement but made no order as to Costs.

NATIONAL FREE CHURCH COUNCIL. MISSIONARY RECEPTION. The National Council of the Evangelical Free Churches will hold a reception of Missionaries of all the Free Church Missionary Societies at the Memorial Hall Farringdon-street, London. Rev. J. Scott Lidgatt, M.A., president of the National Council will receive the guests. The reception will be held in the second week of January 1907. All Mission- ; aries who may be home from their scenes of labour will be invited, the object being to bring, the various Missionary Agencies into closer union. The reception will be preceded by a Conference in the afternoon on active service in the Missionary field. The officials of all the Missionary Societies are expected to be present. The occasion will be a great re-union of Missionary enterprise. Rev. Thomas Law, general secretary of the National Council, is carrying out the arrangements of this new department of the Council's activity which it is antici- pated will become an annual feature. All particulars can be obtained from him at the Memorial Hall, London, E.C.

Widnes St. Paul's 4, Penketh 2. Orford 0, St. tllphin's 7. Albions Reserve 8, Daresbury 0. HOW NELSON'S SAILORS WERE FED. U' vhAvett curioiity to inspect the diet chart of Nelson's sailors: we discover the chief item to be pork, relieved by a mixture of flower and dried fruits, pro- bably currants. Sir Francis Laking says the wholesome and nutritious qualities of Currant Bread (either White or Hovis) is unequalled by any food. Currants, as a matter of fact, contain far more, actual nutriment than lean beef and in a form ready for speedy digestion and assimilation.

In this ease, it is easy. by the in- spection of a hair, to know if the person who furnished it has recently passed through a genera! illness. The hair is made thinner along a part of its length, and the length of the thinned part is proportional to the duration of tl,r, We can, therefore, say whether the illness ha* bben long or short, and almost to a week indi- cate the duration which it has had. That is a fact that may have importance, e.g.. in a ques- tion of identification. From the biological point of view it is, moreover, interesting to find that a hair behaves like the nails'. A now substitute for terra-cotta and plaster is a mixture of forty-five parts of calcined gypsum, thirty of water, ten of albumen, nine of alum, four of sulphate of magnesia, and two of borax. It is stronger and lighter than terra-cotta or plaster. -- Machinery has been invented which rolls iron into such thin sheets that 1.800 of them, piled one upon the other-, are only one inch in thickness. Still, this did not account Messrs. H. J. Westbrook and F. Pearson and the clerk were appointed a deputaton to wait upon the , for Warrington's overwhelming superiority. There is the District Council and Place the resolution before no doubt that the " Wirepullers " have a very fine them. 1 team this season—probably superior to the one which won the Cup—and they should certainly secure a place among the first four in the League. They are trying for League, as well as cup honours, this year and it may interest them to know that an official of a Lancashire club has bet the usual new hat that the championship comes to Warrington. The fact that being in the first four would give them an even chance owing to the knock-out principle then operating strengthens the ground for such a confident opinion. If they do win the League they will I fancy be more entitled to the honours than the present champions were last season.
